Test of Significance for High-dimensional Thresholds with Application to Individualized Minimal Clinically Important Difference
Huijie Feng Jingyi Duan Yang Ning Jiwei Zhao

Abstract
This work is motivated by learning the individualized minimal clinically important difference, a vital concept to assess clinical importance in various biomedical studies. We formulate the scientific question into a high-dimensional statistical problem where the parameter of interest lies in an individualized linear threshold. The goal is to develop a hypothesis testing procedure for the significance of a single element in this parameter as well as of a linear combination of this parameter. The difficulty dues to the high-dimensional nuisance in developing such a testing procedure, and also stems from the fact that this high-dimensional threshold model is nonregular and the limiting distribution of the corresponding estimator is nonstandard. To deal with these challenges, we construct a test statistic via a new bias-corrected smoothed decorrelated score approach, and establish its asymptotic distributions under both null and local alternative hypotheses. We propose a double-smoothing approach to select the optimal bandwidth in our test statistic and provide theoretical guarantees for the selected bandwidth. We conduct simulation studies to demonstrate how our proposed procedure can be applied in empirical studies. We apply the proposed method to a clinical trial where the scientific goal is to assess the clinical importance of a surgery procedure.



Test of Significance for High-dimensional Thresholds with Application to Individualized Minimal Clinically Important Difference

Key Words:

bandwidth selection;
high-dimensional statistical inference;
kernel method;
nonstandard asymptotics.

1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation: Individualized Minimal Clinically Important Difference (iMCID) under High-dimensionality

In clinical studies, instead of statistical significance, the effect of a treatment or intervention is widely assessed through clinical significance.

By leveraging patient-reported outcomes (PRO) that are directly collected from the patients without a third party’s interpretation, the aim of assessing clinical significance is to provide clinicians and policy makers the clinical effectiveness of the treatment or intervention.
For example, in our motivating study, the ChAMP randomized controlled trial
(
Bisson et al. 2015
)
, the interest is to identify
the smallest WOMAC pain score change
such that the corresponding improvement and beyond can be claimed as clinically significant.
In
Jaeschke et al. 1989
, this concept was firstly and formally introduced
as the minimal clinically important difference (MCID), “the smallest difference in score in the domain of interest which patients perceive as beneficial and which would mandate a change in the patient’s management”.

There are roughly three approaches to determine the magnitude of MCID
(
Lassere et al. 2001
;
Erdogan et al. 2016
;
Angst et al. 2017
;
Jayadevappa et al. 2017
)
: distribution-based, opinion-based, and anchor-based. Although adopted in various studies
(
Wyrwich et al. 1999a
;
Wyrwich et al. 1999b
;
Samsa et al. 1999
;
Norman et al. 2003
;
Bellamy et al. 2001
)
, the first two approaches are usually criticized
(
McGlothlin and Lewis 2014
)
due to, for example, “distribution-based methods are not derived from individual patients” and “expert opinion may not be a valid and reliable way to determine what is important to patients”.
The third approach, anchor-based, conceptually determines the MCID by incorporating both certainty of effective treatment encoded as a continuous variable and the patient’s satisfaction collected from the anchor question.
It is clinically evident
(
Wells et al. 2001
)
that the magnitude of MCID would depend on various factors such as the demographic variables and the patients’ baseline status.
For example, in a shoulder pain reduction study
(
Heald et al. 1997
)
, because of the higher expectation for complete recovery, the healthier patients with mild pain at baseline often deemed greater pain reduction as “meaningful” than the ones who suffered from chronic disease.
Therefore, it is of scientific interest to generally estimate the individualized MCID (iMCID) based on each individual patient’s clinical profile as well as to quantify the uncertainties of those estimates.

Nowadays, there is an increasing use and advancing development of EHR-based (electronic health records) studies in clinical research.
The EHR data are complex, diverse and high-dimensional
(
Abdullah et al. 2020
)
.
The rich information contained in the EHR data could facilitate the determination and quantification of iMCID.
Therefore, there is a pressing need to develop statistical methods that incorporate the high-dimensional data into both magnitude determination and uncertainty quantification of iMCID.

1.2
Problem Formulation

To facilitate the presentation, we first introduce some notation.
Let
X\in\mathbb{R}
be a continuous variable representing the score change collected from the PRO, e.g., the WOMAC pain score change from baseline to one year after surgery in the ChAMP trial.
Let
Y=\pm 1
be a binary variable derived from the patient’s response to the anchor question, where
Y=1
represents an improved health condition and
Y=-1
otherwise.
We use a
d
-dimensional vector
\bm{Z}
to denote the patient’s clinical profile including demographic variables, clinical biomarkers, disease histories, among many others.
Suppose the data we observe are
n
i.i.d. samples
\{(x_{i},y_{i},\bm{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
(X,Y,\bm{Z})
.
We focus on the high-dimensional setting, i.e.,
d\gg n
.

Firstly, if there were no covariate
\bm{Z}
, the MCID can be estimated by
\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\tau}\{\mathbb{P}(X\geq\tau\mid Y=1)+\mathbb{P}(X<\tau\mid Y=-1)\}
,
which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\tau}~\mathbb{E}[w(Y)L_{01}\{Y(X-\tau)\}],

(1.1)

where
L_{01}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\{1-\text{sign}(u)\}
is the 0-1 loss,
\text{sign}(u)=1
if
u\geq 0
and
-1
otherwise,
w(1)=1/\pi
,
w(-1)=1/(1-\pi)
and
\pi=\mathbb{P}(Y=1)
.
When the high-dimensional covariate
\bm{Z}
is available, as the focus of this paper, the natural idea is to consider the iMCID with a functional form of
\bm{Z}
, say
\tau(\bm{Z})
. In clinical practice, a simple structure, such as linear, is preferred due to its transparency and convenience for interpretation, especially for high-dimensional data.
Therefore, we focus on the linear structure
\tau(\bm{Z})=\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z}
in this paper.

The objective thus becomes

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\beta}}R(\bm{\beta}),~~\textrm{where}~~R(\bm{\beta})=\mathbb{E}\big[w(Y)L_{01}\{Y(X-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z})\}\big],

(1.2)

and the expectation is with respect to the joint distribution of
(X,Y,\bm{Z})
.

Throughout this paper we assume that
\bm{\beta}^{*}
exists and is unique—the existence and uniqueness can be verified under specific models; see Section S3.1 in the Supplement for details.
Denote
\bm{\beta}^{*}=(\theta^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*T})^{T}
, where
\theta^{*}
is an arbitrary one-dimensional component of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
represents the rest of the parameter which is high-dimensional.
In this paper, we start from considering the hypothesis testing procedure for the parameter
\theta^{*}
. With a simple reparametrization, the same procedure can be applied to infer the iMCID
\bm{c}_{0}^{T}\bm{\beta}^{*}
for some fixed and known vector
\bm{c}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

It is worthwhile to mention that, although the motivation of this paper is to study iMCID, our formulation of this problem can be similarly applied to other scenarios as well, such as the covariate-adjusted Youden index
(
Xu et al. 2014
)
, one-bit compressed sensing
(
Boufounos and Baraniuk 2008
)
, linear binary response model
(
Manski 1975
;
Manski 1985
)
, and personalized medicine
(
Wang et al. 2018
)
. Interested readers could refer to
Feng et al. 2022
for those examples.

1.3
From Estimation to Inference

Incorporating high-dimensional data in the objective, i.e., moving forward from (
1.1
) to (
1.2
), is not trivial, even for the purpose of estimation only.
Recently,
Mukherjee et al. 2019
established the rate of convergence of the (penalized) maximum score estimator for (
1.2
) in growing dimension that
d
is allowed to grow with
n
.
In a related work,
Feng et al. 2022
proposed a regularized empirical risk minimization framework with a smoothed surrogate loss for estimating the high-dimensional parameter
\bm{\beta}^{*}
, and showed the estimation problem is nonregular in that there do not exist estimators of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
with root-
n
convergence rate uniformly over a proper parameter space.

Under (
1.2
), developing a valid statistical inference procedure is challenging, even for fixed dimensional setting.

Manski 1975
;
Manski 1985
considered the binary response model
Y=\text{sign}(X-\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\beta}+\epsilon)
, where
\epsilon
may depend on
(X,\bm{Z})
but with
\text{Median}(\epsilon|X,\bm{Z})=0
. It can be shown that the true coefficient
\bm{\beta}^{*}
can be equivalently defined via (
1.2
) with
w(-1)=w(1)=1/2
. The maximum score estimator is proposed to estimate
\bm{\beta}
, and is later shown to have a non-Gaussian limiting distribution
(
Kim et al. 1990
)
.
To tackle the challenge of nonstandard limiting distribution of the maximum score estimator,
Horowitz 1992
proposed the smoothed maximum score estimator which is asymptotically normal in fixed dimension.

On top of the nonregularity of the problem (
1.2
), the high-dimensionality of the parameter adds an
additional layer of complexity for inference.
The reason is that the estimator that minimizes the penalized loss function does not have a tractable standard limiting distribution under high dimensionality, due to the bias induced by the penalty term.
For regular models (e.g., generalized linear models), there is a growing literature on correcting the bias from the penalty for valid inference, such as
Javanmard and Montanari 2014
;
Zhang and Zhang 2014
;
Van de Geer et al. 2014
;
Belloni et al. 2015
;
Ning and Liu 2017
;
Cai and Guo 2017
;
Fang et al. 2017
;
Neykov et al. 2018
;
Feng and Ning 2019
;
Fang et al. 2020
,
among others.
Their main idea is to firstly construct a consistent estimator of the high dimensional parameter via proper regularization, and then remove the bias (via debiasing or decorrelation) in order to develop valid inferential statistics.
While these methods enjoy great success under regular models, it remains unclear whether they can be applied to conduct valid inference in nonregular models
such as the problem we consider in this paper.
To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first that provides valid inferential tools for nonregular models in high dimension.

1.4
Our Contributions

In this paper, we propose a unified hypothesis testing framework for the one-dimensional parameter
\theta^{*}
as well as for the iMCID encoded as a linear combination of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
. We start from considering the hypothesis testing problem
H_{0}:\theta^{*}=0
versus
H_{1}:\theta^{*}\neq 0
, where we treat
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
as
a high-dimensional nuisance parameter. Built on the smoothed surrogate estimation framework
(
Feng et al. 2022
)
, we propose a bias corrected smoothed decorrelated score to form the score test statistic.

There are several new ingredients in the construction of our score statistic. First, the score function is derived based on a smoothed surrogate loss to overcome the nonregularity due to the nonsmoothness of the 0-1 loss. Second, unlike the existing works on high-dimensional inference, the score function from the smoothed surrogate loss is asymptotically biased. By explicitly estimating the bias term, we derive a new bias corrected score. Third, the decorrelation step, developed by
Ning and Liu 2017
for regular models, is applied to reduce the uncertainty of estimating high-dimensional nuisance parameters. Compared to
Ning and Liu 2017
, the adoption of the smoothed loss and the corresponding bias correction step are new, which also make our inference much more challenging than the existing works. Theoretically, we show that under some conditions, the proposed score test statistic converges in distribution to a standard Gaussian distribution under the null hypothesis. We further establish the local asymptotic power of the test statistic when
\theta^{*}
deviates from
0
in a local neighborhood. In particular, we give the conditions under which the test statistic has asymptotic power one.

When constructing the bias corrected smoothed decorrelated score, we need to specify a bandwidth parameter, whose optimal choice depends on the unknown smoothness of the data distribution. We further propose a double-smoothing approach to select the optimal bandwidth by minimizing the mean squared error (MSE) of the score function. To our knowledge, such bandwidth selection procedures have not been studied for high-dimensional models.
We show that under some extra smoothness assumptions, the ratio of the data-driven bandwidth to the theoretically optimal bandwidth converges to one in probability. Moreover, the proposed score test statistic with the data-driven bandwidth still converges in distribution to a standard Gaussian distribution under the null hypothesis.

1.5
Paper Structure and Notation

The organization of this paper is as follows.
In Section
2
, we first provide some background on the estimation of iMCID then introduce the bias corrected smoothed deccorelated score and the associated test statistic.
In Section
3
, we discuss the theoretical properties of the score test. The data-driven bandwidth selection is addressed in Section
4
.
The corresponding results for
\bm{c}_{0}^{T}\bm{\beta}^{*}
, the linear combination of parameters, are briefly summarized in Section
5
.
Sections
6
and
7
contain simulation studies and a real data example, respectively.
All the technical details and proofs are contained in the Supplement.

Throughout the paper, we adopt the following notation.
For any set
{\mathcal{S}}
, we write
|{\mathcal{S}}|
for its cardinality.
For any vector
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
\bm{v}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
to denote the subvector of
\bm{v}
with entries indexed by the set
{\mathcal{S}}
, and define its
\ell_{q}
norm as
\|\bm{v}\|_{q}=(\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\bm{v}_{j}|^{q})^{1/q}
for some real number
q\geq 0
. For any matrix
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, we denote
||\bm{M}||_{\max}=\max_{i,j}|M_{ij}|
. For any two sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
if there exists some positive constant
C
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for any
n
. We let
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
stand for
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
b_{n}\lesssim a_{n}
. Denote
a\vee b=\max(a,b)
and
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
.
For function
F(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
, we denote
\nabla_{\theta}F(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
and
\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}F(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
as the first order derivatives, and
\nabla^{2}_{\theta,\theta}F(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
the second order derivative.

2
Methodology

2.1
Review of Penalized Smoothed Surrogate Estimation

Under high dimensionality that
d\gg n
, estimating
\bm{\beta}^{*}
via the empirical risk minimization (
1.2
) induces challenges from both statistical and computational perspectives.
The non-smoothness of
L_{01}(u)
would cause the estimator to have a nonstandard convergence rate, which happens even in the fixed low dimensional case
(
Kim et al. 1990
)
.
Moreover, minimizing the empirical risk function based on the 0-1 loss is computationally NP-hard and is often very difficult to implement.
To tackle these challenges,
Feng et al. 2022
considered the following smoothed surrogate risk

\displaystyle R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta})=\mathbb{E}\bigg[w(Y)L_{\delta,K}\big\{Y(X-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z})\big\}\bigg],

(2.1)

where
L_{\delta,K}(u)=\int_{u/\delta}^{\infty}K(t)dt
is a smoothed approximation of
L_{01}(u)
,
K
is a kernel function defined in Section
3
and
\delta>0
is a bandwidth parameter. As the bandwidth
\delta
shrinks to 0,
L_{\delta,K}(u)
converges pointwisely to
L_{01}(u)
(for any
u\neq 0
), from which it can be shown that
\bm{\beta}^{*}
also minimizes the smoothed risk
R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta})
up to a small approximation error. They further proposed the following penalized smoothed surrogate estimator

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\beta}}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta})+P_{\lambda}(\bm{\beta}),

(2.2)

where
P_{\lambda}(\bm{\beta})
is some sparsity inducing penalty (e.g., Lasso) with a tuning parameter
\lambda
, and

R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta})
is the corresponding empirical risk

\displaystyle R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{R}^{i}_{\delta}(\bm{\beta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(y_{i})L_{\delta,K}\Big(y_{i}(x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}_{i})\Big).

(2.3)

Computationally, the empirical surrogate risk
R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta})
is a smooth function of
\bm{\beta}
, which renders the optimization more tractable. Statistically, under some conditions, the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
is shown to be rate-optimal, i.e., the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
matches the minimax lower bound up to a logarithmic factor. We refer to
Feng et al. 2022
for the detailed results.

2.2
Bias Corrected Smoothed Decorrelated Score

While
Feng et al. 2022
showed that the penalized smoothed surrogate estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
is consistent, it does not automatically equip with a practical inferential procedure for
\bm{\beta}^{*}
, mainly because of the sparsity inducing penalty.
In practice, how to draw valid statistical inference is often the ultimate goal. In our motivating example, it is of critical importance to quantify the uncertainty of
\bm{c}_{0}^{T}\bm{\beta}^{*}
where
\bm{c}_{0}
represents the realized value of a new patient’s clinical profile. In other words, we would like to develop a testing procedure for

\displaystyle H_{0L}:\bm{c}_{0}^{T}\bm{\beta}^{*}=0\mbox{ versus }H_{1L}:\bm{c}_{0}^{T}\bm{\beta}^{*}\neq 0.

(2.4)

In this section, we focus on a special case of (
2.4
), the hypothesis test for
\theta^{*}
,

\displaystyle H_{0}:\theta^{*}=0\mbox{ versus }H_{1}:\theta^{*}\neq 0,

(2.5)

where we treat
\bm{\gamma}
as the nuisance parameter.
Once the results for (
2.5
) are clear, we can extend them to (
2.4
), to be presented in Section
5
.

For (
2.5
), we propose a new bias corrected smoothed decorrelated score test.
It is well known that the classical score test is constructed based on the magnitude of the gradient of the loglikelihood, or more generally, the empirical risk function associated with
R(\bm{\beta})
in (
1.2
). However, this construction breaks down in our problem due to the following two reasons.

First, to construct the score statistic, one needs to plug in some estimate of the nuisance parameter
\bm{\gamma}
such as
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}
obtained by partitioning
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}=(\widehat{\theta},\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{T})^{T}
in (
2.2
). However, since
\bm{\gamma}
is a high-dimensional parameter, the estimation error from
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}
may become the leading term in the asymptotic analysis of the score function.
To deal with the high-dimensional nuisance parameter, we use the decorrelated score, where the key idea is to project the score of the parameter of interest to a high-dimensional nuisance space
(
Ning and Liu 2017
)
.
On the population level, it takes the form

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}R(\theta,\bm{\gamma})-\bm{\omega}^{*T}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R(\theta,\bm{\gamma}),

(2.6)

where the decorrelation vector is
\bm{\omega}^{*}=\big(\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\big)^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
.
When
R(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
corresponds to the expected loglikelihood function of the data, the definition of
\bm{\omega}^{*}
coincides with that in
Ning and Liu 2017
.
In general, however,
R(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
is not always the loglikelihood function, so we define
\bm{\omega}^{*}
as
\big(\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\big)^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
in order to mitigate the bias from estimating
\bm{\gamma}
.
We refer to the review paper
(
Neykov et al. 2018
)
for further discussions.

Second, even if the above decorrelated score approach can successfully remove the effect of the high-dimensional nuisance parameter, one cannot construct the sample based decorrelated score from (
2.6
), as the sample version of
R(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
is non-differentiable, leading to the so called non-standard inference. To circumvent this issue, we approximate
R(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
in (
2.6
) by the smoothed surrogate risk
R_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
in (
2.1
), that is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}R(\theta,\bm{\gamma})-\bm{\omega}^{*T}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R(\theta,\bm{\gamma})=\big\{\nabla_{\theta}R_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})-\bm{\omega}^{*T}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})\big\}-\textrm{approximation bias}.

(2.7)

Since the empirical version of
R_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
is smooth, we define the (empirical) smoothed decorrelated score function as
S_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})=\nabla_{\theta}R^{n}_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})-\bm{\omega}^{*T}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R^{n}_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
.
With
\bm{\gamma}
estimated by
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}
, the estimated score function is then naturally defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})=\nabla_{\theta}R^{n}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})-\widehat{\bm{\omega}}^{T}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R^{n}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}),

(2.8)

where
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
, to be defined more precisely in Section
2.3
, is an estimator of
\bm{\omega}^{*}
.

In view of (
2.7
) and (
2.8
), the sample version of
\nabla_{\theta}R_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})-\bm{\omega}^{*T}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
is given by
\widehat{S}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})
and therefore, to construct a valid score function, it remains to estimate the approximation bias in (
2.7
). To proceed,

we first analyze the population version of this approximation bias,

which is simply
\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
at
\bm{\beta}=\bm{\beta}^{*}
, where
\bm{v}^{*}=(1,-\bm{\omega}^{*T})^{T}
. After some analysis, we can show that the magnitude of the approximation bias depends on the smoothness of
f(x|y,\bm{z})
, the conditional density of
X
given
Y
and
\bm{Z}
. To obtain an explicit form of the approximation bias, we assume that
f(x|y,\bm{z})
is
\ell
th order differentiable for some
\ell\geq 2
, to be defined more precisely in Section
3
. Under this assumption, we can show that as the bandwidth parameter
\delta\rightarrow 0
,

\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})=\delta^{\ell}\mu^{*}(1+o(1))
,
where

\displaystyle\mu^{*}:=\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{b}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*T}\Big(\int K(u)\frac{u^{\ell}}{\ell!}du\Big)\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\int y\bm{z}f^{(\ell)}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z},

(2.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Big(\int K(u)\frac{u^{\ell}}{\ell!}du\Big)}_{\gamma_{K,\ell}}\bm{v}^{*T}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Big[w(Y)Y\bm{Z}f^{(\ell)}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{Z}|Y,\bm{Z})\Big]}_{T^{(\ell)}(\bm{\beta}^{*})},

and
f^{(\ell)}(x|y,\bm{z})
denotes the
\ell
th order derivative of
f(x|y,\bm{z})
with respect to
x
.

To estimate the approximation bias
\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
, it suffices to estimate
\mu^{*}
. From (
2.9
), once
f^{(\ell)}(x|y,\bm{z})
at
x=\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}
is estimated, we can construct a plug-in estimator for
\mu^{*}
. To be specific, assume that a pilot kernel estimator with some kernel function
U
and bandwidth
h
is available to estimate
f^{(\ell)}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})
. Then we can estimate
\mu^{*}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}=\gamma_{K,\ell}\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\widehat{T}_{h,U}^{(\ell),n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}),

(2.10)

where
\widehat{T}_{h,U}^{(\ell),n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(y_{i})y_{i}\frac{\bm{z}_{i}}{h^{1+\ell}}U^{(\ell)}\big(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}-x_{i}}{h}\big)

and
\widehat{\bm{v}}=(1,-\widehat{\bm{\omega}}^{T})^{T}
.

The last step to construct a valid score test is to find the asymptotic variance of the smoothed decorrelated score
S_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
. Lemma
1
in the next section shows that the asymptotic variance of the standardized decorrelated score
(n\delta)^{1/2}S_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
is
\sigma^{*2}=\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}
, where

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}^{*}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)^{2}\int\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}\int K(u)^{2}duf(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z},

(2.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Big(\int K(u)^{2}du\Big)}_{\widetilde{\mu}_{K}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Big[w(Y)^{2}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}f(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{Z}|Y,\bm{Z})\Big]}_{H(\bm{\beta}^{*})},

and thus
\sigma^{*}
can be estimated by

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}=\sqrt{\widetilde{\mu}_{K}\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\widehat{H}^{n}_{g,L}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\widehat{\bm{v}}},

(2.12)

where

\widehat{H}_{g,L}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{2}(y_{i})\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{i}^{T}\frac{1}{g}L(\frac{x_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}}{g})

with some kernel function
L
and bandwidth
g
. In Section S4 in the Supplement, we propose an alternative kernel-free estimator of
\sigma^{*}
, which does not require any additional kernel function or bandwidth. We show that the estimator is still consistent for
\sigma^{*}
but may have a slower convergence rate than
\widehat{\sigma}
here.

Equipped with the smoothed decorrelated score
\widehat{S}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})
in (
2.8
), the estimate of the approximation bias
\delta^{\ell}\widehat{\mu}
in (
2.10
) and the estimate of the asymptotic variance
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
in (
2.12
), we define the bias corrected smoothed decorrelated score statistic as

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{n}=\sqrt{n\delta}\Big(\frac{\widehat{S}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})-\delta^{\ell}\widehat{\mu}}{\widehat{\sigma}}\Big).

(2.13)

Remark 1
.

Compared to the existing decorrelated score approach
(
Ning and Liu 2017
)
, our methodological innovation is to develop an explicit bias correction step to remove the approximation bias in (
2.7
) induced by the smoothed surrogate risk. From the theoretical aspect, our test statistic
\widehat{U}_{n}
is rescaled by
(n\delta)^{1/2}
rather than the classical
n^{1/2}
factor, which leads to the non-standard rate of the decorrelated score not only under the null but also under local alternatives; see Section
3
.

2.3
Detailed Implementation

For numerical implementation, we follow the path-following algorithm presented in
Feng et al. 2022
to compute the initial estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
. For the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
, recall that
\bm{\omega}^{*}
satisfies
\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\bm{\omega}^{*}=\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
. Since
\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
can be approximated by the Hessian of the smoothed surrogate loss
\nabla^{2}R^{n}_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
, we consider the following Dantzig type estimator
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
, where

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\omega}}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\omega}}||\bm{\omega}||_{1}~~~~~~~s.t.~~||\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}R^{n}_{\delta}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})-\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}R^{n}_{\delta}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\bm{\omega}||_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{\prime},

(2.14)

for some tuning parameter
\lambda^{\prime}>0
.

For implementing
\widehat{U}_{n}
, we note that the analysis of the asymptotic distribution of
\widehat{U}_{n}
is complicated by the dependence between the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
S_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
. To decouple the dependence and ease theoretical development, we apply the cross-fitting technique to construct the bias corrected smoothed decorrelated score. Specifically, instead of utilizing the same set of samples for estimating
\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
and constructing the score function
S_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
, we will firstly estimate
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
using one set of samples, and then use the rest of samples for estimating
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
and constructing
S_{\delta}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})
.
We can further switch the samples and aggregate the decorrelated score.
Without loss of generality, assume the sample size
n
is even and we divide the samples into two halves with equal size for this purpose. Formally, denote
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(i)},\widehat{\bm{\omega}}^{(i)},i=1,2
as the estimator based on the
i
th fold of the samples,
\mathcal{N}_{i}
, and similarly
\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(i)}}(\bm{\beta}),\nabla^{2}R_{\delta}^{n_{(i)}}(\bm{\beta})
as the corresponding gradient and Hessian. Define

\widehat{S}^{(1)}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})=\nabla_{\theta}R^{n_{(1)}}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})-\widehat{\bm{\omega}}^{(1)T}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R^{n_{(1)}}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)}),

and
\widehat{S}^{(2)}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(1)})
in a similar way. The estimated decorrelated score via cross-fitting is

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})=\frac{1}{2}\big(\widehat{S}^{(1)}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})+\widehat{S}^{(2)}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(1)})\big).

(2.15)

Similarly, we define the cross-fitted estimators
\widehat{\mu}
and
\widehat{\sigma}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{K,\ell}(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)T}\widehat{T}_{h,U}^{(\ell),n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)})+\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(2)T}\widehat{T}^{(\ell),n_{(2)}}_{h,U}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(1)})),

(2.16)

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\mu}_{K}}{2}\bigg[\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)T}\widehat{H}^{n_{(1)}}_{g,K}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)})\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}+\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(2)T}\widehat{H}^{n_{(2)}}_{g,K}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(1)})\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(2)}\bigg],

where

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{h,U}^{(\ell),n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}_{1}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}w(y_{i})y_{i}\frac{\bm{z}_{i}}{h^{1+\ell}}U^{(\ell)}\bigg(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)T}\bm{z}_{i}-x_{i}}{h}\bigg),

(2.17)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{g,L}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}_{1}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}w^{2}(y_{i})\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{i}^{T}\frac{1}{g}L(\frac{x_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)T}\bm{z}_{i}}{g}),

and similarly for

\widehat{T}_{h,U}^{(\ell),n_{(2)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(1)}),\widehat{H}_{g,L}^{n_{(2)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(1)})
. Given
\widehat{S}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})
in (
2.15
) and the above estimators
\widehat{\mu}
and
\widehat{\sigma}
, we can form the score test statistic
\widehat{U}_{n}
in the same way as in (
2.13
).

3
Theory

3.1
Assumptions

In this paper, we consider the following definition of function smoothness.

Definition 1
.

We say the conditional density
f(x|y,\bm{z})
of
X
given
Y,\bm{Z}
is
\ell
th order smooth, if for any
\bm{z}
and
y\in\{-1,1\}
, the conditional density
f(x|y,\bm{z})
is
\ell
-times continuously differentiable in
x
with derivatives
f^{(i)}(x|y,\bm{z})
bounded by a constant
C
,
|f^{(i)}(x|y,\bm{z})|\leq C
for
i=1,\dotso,\ell
, and
f^{(\ell)}(x|y,\bm{z})
is Hölder continuous with some exponent
0<\zeta\leq 1
, that is, for any
\bm{z},\triangle
and
y\in\{-1,1\}
,
|f^{(\ell)}(x+\triangle|y,\bm{z})-f^{(\ell)}(x|y,\bm{z})|\leq L\triangle^{\zeta}
,
where
L>0
is some constant.

Assumption 1
.

We assume
f(x|y,\bm{z})
is
\ell
th order smooth with some integer
\ell\geq 2
.

Assumption
1
concerns the smoothness of
f(x|y,\bm{z})
. To see why the smoothness condition is important, notice that the gradient functions of (
2.1
) and (
1.2
) are

\displaystyle\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta})=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\int yz\Big[\int\frac{1}{\delta}K(\frac{y(x-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z})}{\delta})f(x|y,\bm{z})dx\Big]f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

(3.1)

\displaystyle\nabla R(\bm{\beta})=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\int yzf(\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z},

from which we can see that
f(\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})
in
\nabla R(\bm{\beta})
is substituted by its kernel approximation
\int\frac{1}{\delta}K(\frac{y(x-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z})}{\delta})f(x|y,\bm{z})dx
, and thus the difference between
\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta})
and
\nabla R(\bm{\beta})
naturally depends on the smoothness of
f(x|y,\bm{z})
.

Notice that our smoothness condition in Definition
1
is slightly stronger than the standard Hölder smoothness condition in the nonparametric literature
(
Tsybakov 2009
)
. In particular, we require that
f^{(\ell)}(x|y,\bm{z})
is Hölder continuous with some exponent
0<\zeta\leq 1
. This additional assumption is essential to show the rate of the bias estimator
\widehat{\mu}
in (
2.10
). The Hölder class condition in Assumption
1
can be relaxed to a variation of Nikol’ski class condition
(
Tsybakov 2009
)
; see Section S3.2 in the Supplement for details.

Assumption 2
.

We assume
K(t)
is a kernel function with bounded support that satisfies:
K(t)=K(-t)
,
|K(t)|\leq K_{\max}<\infty\;\forall\;t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\int K(t)dt=1
,
\int K^{2}(t)dt<\infty
, and
|K^{\prime}|<\infty
. We also assume that
K
degenerates at the boundaries. A kernel is said to be of order
\ell\geq 1
if it satisfies

\int t^{j}K(t)dt=0,\;\forall\;j=1,\dotso,\ell-1
,
\int t^{\ell}K(t)dt\neq 0
, and
\int|t|^{q}|K(t)|dt
are bounded by a constant for any
q\in[\ell,\ell+1]
.

Assumption
2
above is about the kernel function
K(t)
that we first introduced in the surrogate risk
R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta})
in (
2.1
).
We provide a list of commonly-seen second-order, fourth-order and sixth-order kernel functions in Section S3.3 of the Supplement.

We now impose regularity conditions on
(X,Y,\bm{Z})
.

Assumption 3
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that
c\leq\mathbb{P}(Y=1)\leq 1-c
and the weight function
w(\cdot)
is positive and upper bounded by a constant.

Assumption 4
.

We assume
\max_{1\leq j\leq d}|Z_{j}|\leq M_{n}
for some
M_{n}
that possibly depends on
n
, where
M_{n}^{2}\leq C\sqrt{n\delta/\log(d)}
for some constant
C>0
.
We also assume that
\mathbb{E}[|Z_{j}|^{4}|Y=y]
is bounded by a constant for
y\in\{1,-1\}
.

Assumption 5
.

We assume
\sigma^{*}=\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}
is bounded away from 0 and infinity by some constants, and
|\mu^{*}|=|\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{b}^{*}|
is also upper bounded by a constant.

Assumption
4
requires the boundedness of
\bm{Z}
and the fourth order moment.

Notice that if each component of
\bm{Z}
is sub-Gaussian with bounded sub-Gaussian norm, Assumption
4
is satisfied with high probability with
M_{n}\asymp\sqrt{\log d}
providing
(\log d)^{3}/(n\delta)=O(1)
which is a mild assumption.
For binary covariates
Z_{j}\in\{0,1\}
, it holds that
M_{n}=1
.

Assumption
5
ensures that the asymptotic variance of the smoothed decorrelated score
\sigma^{*}
does not degenerate and the approximation bias
\mu^{*}
is bounded. In Section S3.4 in the Supplement, we verify that under mild conditions, Assumptions
1
-
5
hold under the binary response model.
Finally, we impose the following assumption on the estimators of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
\bm{\omega}^{*}
.

Assumption 6
.

Assume there are estimators
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{v}}=(1,-\widehat{\bm{\omega}}^{T})^{T}
with

||\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{*}||_{1}\lesssim\eta_{1}(n)~~~~\text{and}~~~~||\widehat{\bm{v}}-\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}/||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\lesssim\eta_{2}(n),

for some non-random sequences
\eta_{1}(n),\eta_{2}(n)
converging to
0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

It is shown by
Feng et al. 2022
that, under some conditions, the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
in (
2.2
) achieves the (near) minimax-optimal rate
\eta_{1}(n)=\sqrt{s}(\frac{s\log(d)}{n})^{\ell/(2\ell+1)}

where
s=\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{0}
. For
\widehat{\bm{v}}=(1,-\widehat{\bm{\omega}}^{T})^{T}
, we assume
||\widehat{\bm{v}}-\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\lesssim||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\eta_{2}(n)
. Notice that the term
||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}
is not absorbed into
\eta_{2}(n)
only for notational simplicity.
In Lemma S7 in the Supplement, we show that a Dantzig type estimator
\widehat{\bm{v}}
could attain the fast rate
\eta_{2}(n)
.

3.2
Theoretical Results

We start from the following lemma which characterizes the asymptotic distribution of the decorrelated score function evaluated at the true parameter
\bm{\beta}^{*}
.

Lemma 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
5
, if
(||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}M_{n})^{3}/(n\delta)^{1/2}=o(1)
and
\delta=o(1)
, then

\displaystyle\sqrt{n\delta}\frac{\bm{v}^{*T}(\nabla R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))}{\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1),

(3.2)

where

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})=\delta^{\ell}\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{b}^{*}(1+o(1)).

(3.3)

Asymptotically, the bias and standard deviation of
\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
can be seen from this lemma.
Since
\mu^{*}=\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{b}^{*}
and
\sigma^{*}=\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}
are both bounded by constants, the asymptotic bias and standard deviation are of order
\delta^{\ell}
and
(n\delta)^{-1/2}
, respectively. Thus, choosing
\delta=cn^{-1/(2\ell+1)}
for any constant
c>0
attains the optimal bias and variance trade-off. Note that in this lemma we require
(||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}M_{n})^{3}/(n\delta)^{1/2}=o(1)
to verify the Lindeberg condition in the central limit theorem, which holds as long as
\delta
does not shrink to zero too fast.

Our first main theorem characterizes the asymptotic normality of the decorrelated score under the null hypothesis with nuisance parameters
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
and
\bm{\omega}^{*}
estimated by those in Assumption
6
.

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
6
, if
(||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}M_{n})^{3}/(n\delta)^{1/2}=o(1)
,
\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta^{3}}=o(1)
,
n\delta^{2\ell+1}=O(1)
, and

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\bigg(\frac{\eta_{1}(n)}{\delta}\vee\eta_{2}(n)\bigg)\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}\vee\delta^{\ell}\vee M_{n}^{2}\eta_{1}(n)\bigg)=o(1),

(3.4)

then under
H_{0}:\theta^{*}=0
, it holds that

\frac{\sqrt{n\delta}\widehat{S}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})-\sqrt{n\delta^{2\ell+1}}\mu^{*}}{\sigma^{*}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1)
.

Theorem
1
implies that the decorrelated score with some high-dimensional plug-in estimators
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
has the same asymptotic distribution as in Lemma
1
. Several conditions are needed to show this result. The first condition
(||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}M_{n})^{3}/(n\delta)^{1/2}=o(1)
is from Lemma
1
, and the second condition
\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta^{3}}=o(1)
is also mild as long as
\delta
does not go to zero too fast. The third condition
n\delta^{2\ell+1}=O(1)
guarantees that the higher order bias of the decorrelated score can be ignored and therefore it suffices to only correct for the leading bias term in (
3.3
).

We now elaborate the condition (
3.4
). Roughly speaking, the term
\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}\vee\delta^{\ell}\vee M_{n}^{2}\eta_{1}(n)
comes from the bound for
\|\nabla R^{n_{(1)}}_{\delta}(\theta,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})-\nabla R(\theta,{\bm{\gamma}})\|_{\infty}
. Indeed, the cross-fitting technique guarantees the independence between
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)}
and
\nabla R^{n_{(1)}}_{\delta}(\theta,{\bm{\gamma}})
, which plays a key role in the analysis. Condition (
3.4
) simply means that this bound interacting with the estimation error of
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
is sufficiently small.
We can further simplify the condition (
3.4
) by plugging the order of
\eta_{1}(n)
derived in
Feng et al. 2022
and
\eta_{2}(n)=s^{\prime}(\log(d)/n)^{(\ell-1)/(2\ell+1)}
derived from Lemma S7 in the Supplement where
s^{\prime}=\|\bm{\omega}^{*}\|_{0}
.

Recall that in our score statistic
\widehat{U}_{n}
in (
2.13
), we plug in the estimators
\widehat{\mu}
and
\widehat{\sigma}
for
\mu^{*}
and
\sigma^{*}
. In Lemmas S8 and S9 in the Supplement, we establish the rate of convergence of
\widehat{\mu}
and
\widehat{\sigma}
. Under the assumption that
|\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{*}|=o_{p}(1)
and
|\widehat{\sigma}-\sigma^{*}|=o_{p}(1)
, the Slutsky’s theorem implies that the bias corrected decorrelated score statistic
\widehat{U}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1)
under the null hypothesis.

Accordingly, given the desired significance level
\alpha
, we define the test function as

\displaystyle T_{DS}=I(|\widehat{U}_{n}|>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)),

where
\Phi^{-1}(\cdot)
is the inverse function of the cdf of the standard normal distribution. Thus, our result shows that the Type I error of the test
T_{DS}
converges to
\alpha
asymptotically, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(T_{DS}=1|H_{0})\rightarrow\alpha
.

Now denote

\nabla^{2}_{\theta|\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})=\nabla^{2}_{\theta\theta}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla^{2}_{\theta\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})(\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma}\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma}\bm{\theta}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
.
Our second main theorem characterizes the limiting behavior of
\widehat{U}_{n}
under the local alternative hypothesis
H_{1}:\theta^{*}=\widetilde{C}n^{-\phi}
for some constants
\widetilde{C}\neq 0
and
\phi>0
.

Theorem 2
.

Assume the conditions in Theorem
1
and in Lemmas S8 and S9 of the Supplement, and further

\displaystyle

\displaystyle||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}^{2}M_{n}^{4}n^{1-4\phi}/\delta=o(1),\quad(n\delta)^{1/2}||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}(\eta_{1}(n)\vee\eta_{2}(n))M_{n}n^{-\phi}=o(1),

(3.5)

and that
\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\sigma}
are consistent estimators of
\mu^{*},\sigma^{*}
. Then, by choosing the optimal bandwidth
\delta\asymp n^{-1/(2\ell+1)}
, the following results hold under the local alternative hypothesis
H_{1}:\theta^{*}=\widetilde{C}n^{-\phi}
.

1.

When
\phi=\frac{\ell}{2\ell+1}
, it holds that

\widehat{U}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(-\xi,1)
,
where
\xi=\widetilde{C}\nabla^{2}_{\theta|\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})/\sigma^{*}
is assumed to be a constant.

2.

When
\phi<\frac{\ell}{2\ell+1}
, it holds that for any fixed
t
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(|\widehat{U}_{n}|>t)=1
.

In addition to the conditions imposed in Theorem
1
and Lemmas S8 and S9, we further require two additional conditions involving the magnitude of
\theta^{*}
in (
S12
). The first condition
||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}^{2}M_{n}^{4}n^{1-4\phi}/\delta=o(1)
is imposed to ensure the local asymptotic normality (LAN) in terms of the parameter
\theta^{*}
.
The second condition in (
S12
) is similar to (
3.4
), which controls
the magnitude of
||\nabla R(0,\bm{\gamma}^{*})||_{\infty}
and
||\nabla^{2}R_{\delta}(\theta^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*})-\nabla^{2}R_{\delta}(0,\bm{\gamma}^{*})||_{\max}
under the alternative hypothesis.

This theorem implies that the proposed test converges in distribution to a normal distribution with mean
-\xi
, when the contiguous alternatives approach the null hypothesis at a rate
n^{-\frac{\ell}{2\ell+1}}
. In addition, if the alternatives deviate from the null hypothesis in the magnitude larger than
n^{-\frac{\ell}{2\ell+1}}
(i.e.,
\phi<\frac{\ell}{2\ell+1}
), the asymptotic power of our test is 1. In other words, our test can successfully detect the nonzero
\theta^{*}
whose magnitude exceeds the order of
n^{-\ell/(2\ell+1)}
. In contrast, for regular models, the local alternative that is detectable is of the standard parametric rate
n^{-1/2}
.

Remark 2
.

By choosing the optimal bandwidth
\delta\asymp n^{-1/(2\ell+1)}
, all the conditions in Theorem
2
can be simplified and summarized as

\displaystyle M_{n}^{2}\leq n^{\ell/(2\ell+1)}(\log d)^{-1/2},\log d=o(n^{(2\ell-2)/(2\ell+1)}),||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}M_{n}^{2}=o(n^{2\phi-(\ell+1)/(2\ell+1)}),\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}M_{n}=o(n^{\ell/(6\ell+3)}\wedge n^{\phi-\ell/(2\ell+1)}\{\eta_{1}(n)\vee\eta_{2}(n)\}^{-1}),

where
\eta_{1}(n)=s^{(4\ell+1)/(4\ell+2)}(\log d/n)^{\ell/(2\ell+1)}=o(1)
,
\eta_{2}(n)=s^{\prime}(\log d/n)^{(\ell-1)/(2\ell+1)}=o(1)
and
||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\{(\log d)^{1/2}\vee n^{\ell/(2\ell+1)}M_{n}^{2}\eta_{1}(n)\}\{n^{1/(2\ell+1)}\eta_{1}(n)\vee\eta_{2}(n)\}=o(1)
.
Consider the extreme case
\ell\to\infty
, it can be verified that
\log d=n^{1/5}
,
s=s^{\prime}=n^{1/10}
,
||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}=n^{1/20}
,
M_{n}=n^{1/50}
would satisfy all of these conditions when
\phi=1/2
.

In addition, these conditions could be further simplified if one is willing to assume
||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}=O(1)
and
M_{n}=O(1)
.
If that is the case, condition (
3.4
) becomes

\displaystyle

\displaystyle s^{(4\ell+1)/(4\ell+2)}(s^{(4\ell+1)/(4\ell+2)}\vee s^{\prime})n^{-(\ell-1)/(2\ell+1)}(\log d)^{(4\ell-1)/(4\ell+2)}=o(1)

when taking
\delta\asymp n^{-1/(2\ell+1)}
. If we consider the extreme case with
\ell\rightarrow\infty
,
it suffices to have
s(s\vee s^{\prime})\log d=o(n^{1/2})
in order to satisfy all of the conditions when
\phi=1/2
.

4
Data-Driven Bandwidth Selection

In the previous section, we establish the theoretical property of the bias corrected smoothed decorrelated score when the underlying conditional density
f(x|y,\bm{z})
is
\ell
th order smooth. However, this smoothness parameter
\ell
is typically unknown in practice, leading to the following two complications. First, in Assumption
2
, a kernel function
K
of the same order is applied, which implicitly requires the knowledge on the smoothness parameter
\ell
. In practice, the choice of kernel functions is often determined by the user’s preference rather than the theory. Since high order kernels may exacerbate the problem of variability, choosing low order kernels of 2 or 4 is often recommended (even if the density is more smooth); see
Härdle et al. 1992
. Second, the optimal bandwidth
\delta\asymp n^{-1/(2\ell+1)}
that balances the asymptotic bias and variance of the decorrelated score in Lemma
1
also depends on the unknown
\ell
. It is well known from the nonparametric literature that the choice of bandwidth is an extremely important problem of both theoretical and practical values
(
Silverman 1986
;
Bowman 1984
;
Sheather and Jones 1991
;
Hall et al. 1992
;
Jones et al. 1996
)
.

In this section, we focus on how to choose the bandwidth
\delta
in a data-driven manner. In view of the above discussion on the kernels, we assume that a low order kernel
K
is chosen (for simplicity, we still denote its order by
\ell
) and meanwhile the underlying conditional density has a higher order smoothness parameter.

Assumption 7
.

We assume that the kernel
K
is of order
\ell
and
f(x|y,\bm{z})
is
(\ell+r)
th order smooth for some
\ell\geq 2
and
r>0
.

We define the optimal bandwidth
\delta^{*}
as the one that minimizes the MSE of the smoothed decorrelated score:

\displaystyle\delta^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\delta}M(\delta),~~\textrm{where}~~M(\delta)=\mathbb{E}[(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}].

(4.1)

A direct bias-variance decomposition of
M(\delta)
gives

\displaystyle M(\delta)=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{1}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}]+\frac{n-1}{n}(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}:=\frac{1}{n}V(\delta)+\frac{n-1}{n}SB(\delta),

(4.2)

where
\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{1}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
is defined in (
2.3
),
V(\delta)=\mathbb{E}[(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{1}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}]
is used as a proxy for the variance, and
SB(\delta)=(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}
denotes the squared error.
To estimate
\delta^{*}
, our main idea is to construct estimators
\widehat{V}(\delta)
and
\widehat{SB}(\delta)
for
V(\delta)
and
SB(\delta)
and then estimate
\delta^{*}
by

\widehat{\delta}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\delta}\widehat{M}(\delta)~~\textrm{where}~~\widehat{M}(\delta)=\frac{1}{n}\widehat{V}(\delta)+\frac{n-1}{n}\widehat{SB}(\delta).

From the proof of Lemma
1
, we can show that
{SB}(\delta)=(\delta^{\ell}\mu^{*})^{2}(1+o(1))
and
{V}(\delta)=\delta^{-1}\sigma^{*2}(1+o(1))
as
\delta\rightarrow 0
, and thus
\sigma^{*2}/\delta
and
(\delta^{\ell}\mu^{*})^{2}
are the asymptotic versions of
V(\delta)
and
SB(\delta)
, respectively.
As a result, one may attempt to estimate the optimal bandwidth by minimizing the asymptotic MSE
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}/\delta+(\delta^{\ell}\widehat{\mu})^{2}
with the plug-in estimators
\widehat{\sigma}
and
\widehat{\mu}
developed in the previous section. However, the asymptotic MSE depends on the unknown smoothness
\ell
and therefore is not appropriate for bandwidth selection in practice.

Instead, we propose to estimate
V(\delta)
and
SB(\delta)
using a different strategy.
Our estimates are still in the cross-fitting fashion, but when we estimate the bias
B(\delta)
that dues to the approximation using the kernel function
K(\cdot)
of order
\ell
with bandwidth
\delta
, we will have to use a new pilot kernel function
J(\cdot)
of order
r
with bandwidth
b
.
Essentially when the target function has higher order smoothness than the kernel function applied for estimation, a different kernel smoothing procedure has to be used for estimating the bias.
This is motivated by the “double-smoothing” technique in nonparametric statistics
(
Härdle et al. 1992
;
Neumann et al. 1995
)
, and is also related to the “smoothed cross validation” approach
(
Hall et al. 1992
)
.

To be specific, we estimate
V(\delta)
with the following moment estimator

\displaystyle\widehat{V}(\delta)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)T}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1)}(\delta)\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}+\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(2)T}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(2)}(\delta)\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(2)}\big),

(4.3)

where
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1)}(\delta)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}_{1}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)})\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)})^{T}
and similarly for
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(2)}
.
To estimate the squared bias
SB(\delta)
, note that the bias term
B(\delta)
can be written as

\displaystyle B(\delta)=\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})=\bm{v}^{*T}\underbrace{(\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))}_{A(\bm{\beta}^{*},\delta)}=\int_{u}K(u)\biggl\{\bm{v}^{*T}(\nabla R(u\delta,\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))\biggr\}du,

(4.4)

where we use
\nabla R(u\delta,\bm{\beta}^{*})=\sum_{y}w(y)\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{z}yf(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}
to denote the population gradient with a bias induced by
u\delta
, with a bit abuse of notation.
We propose to estimate
B(\delta)
by

\displaystyle\widehat{B}(\delta)=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)T}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}_{1}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}A_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)},\delta)+\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(2)T}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}_{2}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{2}}A_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(1)},\delta)\bigg),

(4.5)

where

\displaystyle A_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\delta)=

\displaystyle\int_{u}K(u)w(y_{i})\frac{\bm{z}_{i}y_{i}}{b}\Big[J(\frac{x_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}-u\delta}{b})-J(\frac{x_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}}{b})\Big]du,

(4.6)

and
J(\cdot)
is the aforementioned new pilot kernel function of order
r
with bandwidth
b
. Essentially, we substitute
\nabla R(u\delta,\bm{\beta}^{*})
and
\nabla R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
in (
4.4
) with their corresponding second smoothers through kernel function
J(\cdot)
.
We now estimate the squared bias by
\widehat{SB}(\delta)=\widehat{B}(\delta)^{2}
.

To analyze theoretical properties of the estimates, let’s define
\Delta=[q_{1}n^{-1+\epsilon_{1}},q_{2}n^{-1+\epsilon_{2}}]
as the range of bandwidth
\delta
for some constants

0<q_{1}\leq q_{2}
and
0<\epsilon_{1}<\epsilon_{2}<1
. Since the optimal bandwidth
\delta^{*}
is of order
n^{-1/(2\ell+1)}
, we can guarantee
\delta^{*}\in\Delta
for some suitable
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
. Under some conditions, the uniform convergence rates of
\widehat{V}(\delta)
and
\widehat{SB}(\delta)
are given by

|\widehat{V}(\delta)-V(\delta)|\lesssim\frac{\psi_{1}(n,\delta)}{\delta},~~~~|\widehat{SB}(\delta)-SB(\delta)|\lesssim\delta^{2\ell}\psi_{2}(n,\delta),

uniformly over all
\delta\in\Delta
, where

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(n,\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}^{2}\bigg(\eta_{2}(n)\vee\sqrt{\frac{\log(n\vee d)}{n\delta}}\vee M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)\bigg),

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(n,\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\log(n\vee d)}{nb^{2\ell+1}}}\vee(\delta\vee b)^{r}\vee M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)(1\vee\frac{M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)}{\delta^{\ell}})\vee\eta_{2}(n)\bigg).

We refer to Lemmas S11 and S12 in Section S2 in the Supplement for the formal statement of the results and further interpretations of the rates.
With these two lemmas, we further establish the convergence rate of the data-driven bandwidth
\widehat{\delta}
, i.e.,
\frac{\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*}}{\delta^{*}}\lesssim C_{n,\delta^{*}}
, where
C_{n,\delta^{*}}=\psi_{1}(n,\delta^{*})\vee\psi_{2}(n,\delta^{*})
, see Theorem S2 in the Supplement. That is, our data-driven bandwidth
\widehat{\delta}
is consistent.

For notational simplicity, let
\widehat{U}_{n}(\delta)
denote the bias corrected smoothed decorrelated score test statistic with a pre-specified bandwidth parameter
\delta
. The main result in this section shows that
\widehat{U}_{n}(\widehat{\delta})
with the data-driven bandwidth
\widehat{\delta}
is still asymptotically normal under the null hypothesis.

Theorem 3
.

Under the conditions in Theorem S3 in the Supplement and
H_{0}:\theta^{*}=0
, it holds that

\widehat{U}_{n}({\widehat{\delta}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1).

There are two major challenges in the analysis of
\widehat{U}_{n}({\widehat{\delta}})
. First, the estimator
\widehat{\delta}
and the decorrelated score statistic are generally dependent with each other, which prevents the direct use of many concentration inequalities such as Bernstein’s. To decouple the dependence, similar to Section
2.3
, we carefully design a cross-fitting approach by splitting the data into three folds. Due to the space constraint, we leave the detailed algorithm to Section S2 in the Supplement.
Second, different from Theorem
1
which presents the asymptotic normality of
\widehat{U}_{n}({{\delta}})
with a fixed
\delta
,
the uncertainty of
\widehat{\delta}
needs to be incorporated in the proof of Theorem
3
. In particular, we use concentration inequalities to take care of the higher order error terms in the Taylor expansion of
\widehat{U}_{n}({\widehat{\delta}})
with respect to both
\delta
and
\bm{\beta}
. This leads to much more involved analysis than that in Theorem
1
.

5
Hypothesis Testing for the Linear Combination

Our results presented thus far are for the hypothesis testing (
2.5
), where
\theta
is simply a single element in
\bm{\beta}
. In applications, researchers may be more interested in the hypothesis testing (
2.4
), a linear combination of the parameter
\bm{\beta}
. For example, as we mentioned at the beginning of Section
2.2
, in the study of inferring iMCID, it is of interest to test
\bm{c}_{0}^{T}\bm{\beta}^{*}=0
where
\bm{c}_{0}
represents the realized value of a new patient’s clinical profile and we assume
c_{01}\neq 0
without loss of generality.
The methods and results for (
2.5
) are essential.
Below, we show that, in a parallel manner, all of them can be developed for (
2.4
) which is more applicable in scientific applications.
We also point out, technically, our methods and results can be further extended to a more general null hypothesis
H_{0M}:\mathbf{M}\bm{\beta}^{*}={\bm{0}}
where
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
and
m
is a fixed integer.

To test the hypothesis (
2.4
), consider the one to one reparametrization
(\theta,\bm{\gamma})\rightarrow(\xi,\bm{\gamma})
, where
\xi=\bm{c}_{0}^{T}\bm{\beta}
. Under this new set of parameters, the null hypothesis can be written as
H_{0L}:\xi^{*}=0
, and the smoothed surrogate loss reduces to
R^{n}_{\delta}(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma})
, where we write
\bm{c}_{0}=(c_{01},\bm{c}_{02}^{T})^{T}
with
\bm{c}_{02}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.
Define
\bm{C}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{c_{01}}&0\\[3.00003pt]
\frac{-\bm{c}_{02}}{c_{01}}&\bm{I}_{d-1}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
From the chain rule, we can show that

\displaystyle\nabla_{(\xi,\bm{\gamma})}R^{n}_{\delta}(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma})=\bm{C}\nabla R_{\delta}^{n}(\theta,\bm{\gamma}),~~\nabla^{2}_{(\xi,\bm{\gamma}),(\xi,\bm{\gamma})}R^{n}_{\delta}(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma})=\bm{C}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta}^{n}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})\bm{C}^{T},

(5.1)

and similarly for
R_{\delta}(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma})
and
R(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma})
.
Therefore, following the same idea as in Section
2.2
, we define the smoothed decorrelated score as

\displaystyle S^{L}_{\delta}(\xi,\bm{\gamma},\bm{\omega}^{*}_{L})=\nabla_{\xi}R^{n}_{\delta}(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma})-\bm{\omega}^{*T}_{L}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}R^{n}_{\delta}(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma}),

(5.2)

where

\bm{\omega}^{*}_{L}=\Big[\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}R(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma})\Big]^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\xi}R(\frac{\xi-\bm{c}_{02}^{T}\bm{\gamma}}{c_{01}},\bm{\gamma}).

We write
\bm{v}^{*}_{L}=(1,\bm{\omega}^{*T}_{L})^{T}
and denote
\mu^{*}_{L},\sigma^{*}_{L}
as the (scaled) asymptotic bias and standard deviation of the score function
S^{L}_{\delta}(\xi,\bm{\gamma},\bm{\omega}^{*}_{L})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mu^{*}_{L}=\bm{v}^{*T}_{L}\bm{C}\bm{b}^{*},~~~~~~\sigma^{*}_{L}=\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}_{L}\bm{C}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{C}^{T}\bm{v}^{*}_{L}},

(5.3)

where
\bm{b}^{*}
and
\bm{\Sigma}^{*}
are defined in (
2.9
) and (
2.11
), respectively. From above we can see that the estimation methods for
\bm{\omega}^{*},\mu^{*}
and
\sigma^{*}
proposed in Section
2.2
can be easily extended to obtain corresponding estimators for

\bm{\omega}^{*}_{L},\mu^{*}_{L}
and
\sigma^{*}_{L}
.
Given these estimators, we define the test statistics for
H_{0L}
as

\widehat{U}^{L}_{n}=(n\delta)^{1/2}\frac{S^{L}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}},\widehat{\bm{\omega}}_{L})-\delta^{\ell}\widehat{\mu}_{L}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{L}}.

Accordingly, all the parallel results presented in Section
3
and Section
4
can be developed. In the interest of space, we only present the following result that
characterizes the asymptotic distribution of
\widehat{U}_{n}^{L}
under the null hypothesis
H_{0L}
. All other parallel results are omitted.

Theorem 4
.

If Assumptions
1
-
6
hold with
\mu^{*},\sigma^{*},\bm{v}^{*},\widehat{\bm{v}}
substituted by
\mu^{*}_{L},\sigma^{*}_{L},\bm{v}^{*}_{L},\widehat{\bm{v}}_{L}
, and in addition
\widehat{\mu}_{L}
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{L}
are consistent estimators of
\mu^{*}_{L}
and
\sigma^{*}_{L}
, respectively, then under the same conditions as in Theorem
1
and the null hypothesis
H_{0L}:\xi^{*}=0
, it holds that

\widehat{U}_{n}^{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1)
.

6
Simulation Studies

In this section, we evaluate the empirical performance of the proposed methods. Although many models can be formulated as special cases of our problem (
1.2
), here we mainly consider the following binary response model

\displaystyle Y=\text{sign}(X-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{Z}+\epsilon),

(6.1)

where
\epsilon
possibly depends on
X
and
\bm{Z}
but the median of
\epsilon
given
X
and
\bm{Z}
is 0.

6.1
Experiments with pre-specified bandwidth

In the first set of experiments, we evaluate the performance of the proposed test statistic with pre-specified bandwidth.
We use Gaussian kernel
K
of order 2 with bandwidth pre-specified at
\delta=n^{-1/5}
. The choices of other tuning parameters are detailed in Section S5 in the Supplement.
Throughout this subsection, we consider sample size
n=800
, dimension
d=100,500,1000
and generate
\beta^{*}_{2},\dotso,\beta^{*}_{s}
by sampling from a uniform distribution within
[1,2]
for
s=3,10
. The first coordinate
\beta_{1}^{*}
would vary depending on the purpose of the experiment, and the rest coordinates of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
are all set to 0. After that, the coefficient vector is then normalized such that
||\bm{\beta}^{*}||_{2}=1
. We generate
X\sim N(0,1)
and
\bm{Z}\sim N(0,\bm{\Sigma}_{\rho})
, where
(\bm{\Sigma}_{\rho})_{jk}=\rho^{|j-k|}
with
\rho=0.2,0.5,0.7
. For all cases, the simulations are repeated 250 times.

In the first scenario, we let
\epsilon\sim N(0,0.2^{2}(1+2(X-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{Z})^{2}))
, which is referred to as Heteroskedastic Gaussian scenario later on.
We compare the proposed smoothed decorrelated score test (SDS) with the decorrelated score test method (DS)
(
Ning and Liu 2017
)
and Honest confidence region method (Honest)
(
Belloni et al. 2016
)
from the “hdm” package.
We fix the significance level at
0.05
and firstly evaluate the performance of the tests under the null hypothesis
H_{0}:\beta^{*}_{1}=0
. In this case, we set
\beta^{*}_{1}=0
.
Note that the R code for the DS and Honest approaches is tailored for the high-dimensional logistic regression, which differs from the above data generating process.

Table
6.1
reports the empirical Type I error rate under the first scenario. The error rate from the SDS method is generally close to the nominal significance level 0.05, which empirically verifies the theoretical results in Theorem
1
. For both Honest and DS methods, the empirical Type I error rate seems to be consistently higher or lower than the nominal level. This is expected as these two methods only work for the logistic regression.
By taking a closer look at the Normal Q-Q plot of the test statistics, we observe that the distribution of the test statistics from the Honest and DS methods deviate substantially from Gaussian, as opposed to those yield by the proposed SDS method. Please see Section S6.2 in the Supplement for more details.

In the second scenario, we let
\epsilon\sim 0.2\cdot\text{Unif}(-G(X,\bm{Z}),G(X,\bm{Z}))
, where
G(x,\bm{z})=\sqrt{1+2(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}
. In other words, the error
\epsilon
follows a uniform distribution such that its range depends on the covariates
X,\bm{Z}
(we will call it Heteroskedastic Uniform scenario). Similar to the Heteroskedastic Gaussian case, we compare SDS method with DS and Honest methods and study the empirical Type I error rate. From Table
6.2
we can see that the proposed method yields Type I error close to the nominal level as opposed to the other two. The Normal QQ-plots in Section S6.2 in the Supplement further confirm the asymptotic normality of our SDS test statistics. The above results suggest that in practice, if the underlying data generating process is the binary response model, our proposed approach provides valid inferential results while the existing approaches fail.

Next, we investigate the empirical power of the SDS method. We use the same data generating processes as in the above two scenarios, but instead of setting
\beta^{*}_{1}=0
, we vary
\beta^{*}_{1}
in the grid
\{0.02,0.05,0.075,0.10,0.15,0.20,0.25,0.30\}
for the Heteroskedastic Gaussian case, and
\{0.025,0.05,0.075,0.10,0.125,0.15,0.175\}
for the Heteroskedastic Uniform case. Similarly, we consider
s=3,10
,
d=100,500,1000
and
\rho=0.2,0.5,0.7
. Figure
6.1
shows the empirical rejection rate of the SDS method when
s=10
(see Section S6.3 for the results when
s=3
). Note that we do not compare with the DS and Honest methods for the empirical power, because these two tests do not maintain the desired Type I error in our scenarios. We can see that for all considered cases, the empirical power converges to 1 as the magnitude of the signal
\beta^{*}_{1}
becomes larger, which agrees with Theorem
2
. In addition, we find that the dimension
d
has minor effects on the empirical power, which is reasonable as Theorem
2
only depends on
\log d
via the condition (
S12
). Finally, we note that the power of the test deteriorates as the correlation of the design increases.

Figure 6.1:
Empirical rejection rate of the proposed test under both scenarios with
s=10
,
d=100,500,1000
and
\rho=0.2,0.5,0.7
.

6.2
Experiments with data-driven bandwidth

In the next set of experiments, we study the empirical performance of the data-driven bandwidth selection approach.
We firstly study the type I error and power of our SDS method for testing
H_{0}:\beta^{*}_{1}=0
versus
H_{1}:\beta^{*}_{1}\neq 0
with data-driven bandwidth.
We consider the same data generating processes as in Section
6.1
with
n=800,d=100,s=3,10
and
\rho=0.2,0.5,0.7
. We seek for the minimizer of the estimated MSE over
\delta\in[0.1,1.2]
and each experiment is repeated 250 times. After
\widehat{\delta}
is obtained, we plug-in it into the test statistic and estimate the bias and variance as discussed in Section S5 in the Supplement. With the same implementations, we also evaluate the empirical power of the test by varying
\beta^{*}_{1}
in the same grid as in Section
6.1
.

\widehat{\delta}
.

\widehat{\delta}
.

Table
S13.1
shows the empirical Type I error rate over 250 repetitions when
\beta^{*}_{1}=0
, and Figure
S13.4
shows the empirical power for different
\beta^{*}_{1}\neq 0
in these scenarios. Similar to the case when the bandwidth
\delta
is pre-specified, the empirical Type I errors are generally close to the nominal level 0.05, and the empirical power converges to 1 as
\beta^{*}_{1}
becomes larger. We refer to Section S6 in the Supplement for further numerical results.

From all of these numerical results, we recommend using the data-driven bandwidth selection approach in practice.

7
Analysis of ChAMP Trial

In this section we analyze the ChAMP (Chondral Lesions And Meniscus Procedures) trial
(
Bisson et al. 2017
)
, which contains clinical information about
n=138
patients undergoing arthroscopic partial meniscectomy (APM), a knee surgery for meniscal tears. The response variable is
Y=1
if the patient is healthy/satisfactory and
-1
otherwise, obtained from the SF-36 survey. The continuous measurement
X
encodes the WOMAC pain score change from the baseline to one-year after the surgery.
The dataset also contains
d=160
additional variables from the patient’s clinical profile, denoted by
\bm{Z}
. The scientific question is to determine the iMCID, defined as a linear combination of the covariates
\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z}
, such that the treatment of debriding chondral lesions can be claimed as clinically significant by comparing the WOMAC pain score change with this individualized threshold. As we can see, this application naturally fits into our formulation (
1.2
) with weight function
w(y)=1/\mathbb{P}(Y=y)
. The goal of the analysis is to address this question by providing valid inferential results for each component of
\bm{\beta}
.

We apply the proposed SDS test for
H_{0j}:\beta_{j}=0
versus
H_{1j}:\beta_{j}\neq 0
, where
1\leq j\leq d
. We use the same tuning parameter setting for estimating
\bm{\beta}^{*},\omega^{*}
and the asymptotic bias and variance of the score function following Section S5. For comparison, we also apply the DS and Honest methods discussed in Section
6.1
.

Table 7.1:
The three significant variables (with p-value
<0.05/d
) identified by the proposed SDS method, and their corresponding p-values obtained from the DS and Honest methods.

p-value

KQOL_6wk

flex_inj_pre

KSymp_3mo

SDS

3.583e-07

5.575e-05

4.482e-05

DS

0.0168

0.0340

0.0213

Honest

0.0591

0.0241

0.4383

Table
7.1
lists the three significant variables (i.e., those with p-value
<0.05/d=
3.125e-04) from the proposed SDS approach.
Interestingly, all of them are clinically relevant and can provide meaningful implications for iMCID. The significance of the variable KQOL_6wk, which represents the KOOS score for quality of life at 6-week, definitely indicates how the patients recover at a relatively early stage after the surgery.
The variable flex_inj_pre means the degree of flexion right before the surgery. Its significance recommends that the baseline disease severity would affect the magnitude of iMCID—this similar phenomenon was also discovered in the clinical literature for other types of diseases, such as the shoulder pain reduction study
(
Heald et al. 1997
)
.
The third variable KSymp_3mo is the KOOS score for other symptoms at 3-month. In some previous analysis of ChAMP trial where only estimate is available but without inference results, this variable has the second largest coefficient
(
Feng et al. 2022
)
.

The results from the DS and the Honest methods are different.
Firstly, the DS method only yields 1 significant variable and the Honest method yields 13. From Table
7.1
, the three significant variables identified by the proposed SDS method cannot be identified by either DS or Honest.
In general, compared to the proposed SDS method, DS identifies fewer significant variables while Honest identifies more. This phenomenon is also evident from the simulation results in Section
6.1
.
On the other hand, the results from the three methods do not completely contradict with each other. For instance, the two significant variables, KQOL_6wk and KSymp_3mo, identified from the proposed SDS method, has the fourth and fifth smallest p-values in the DS method.
Please refer to Section S6.7 in the Supplement for more results of this analysis.

In general, recall that DS and Honest methods are devised for the logistic regression, while our
proposed SDS method can produce valid inference results under the binary response model (
6.1
), which is more flexible since the distribution of
\epsilon
is left unspecified.
Therefore, we expect that the significant variables identified by SDS are potentially more reliable and clinically more relevant. Our results presented in this session echo this rationale.

Supplemental Material

The Supplement include the technical proofs, some more detailed theoretical results and discussions, and additional numerical results.

Supplemental Material for “Test of Significance for High-dimensional Thresholds with Application to Individualized Minimal Clinically Important Difference”

S8
Proofs of Main Results

S8.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Recall that

\displaystyle\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})=w(y_{i})\frac{y_{i}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}K\bigg(\frac{y_{i}(x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i})}{\delta}\bigg).

(S1)

Let
T_{i}=\frac{\sqrt{\delta}\bm{v}^{*T}(\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))}{\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}}
, we know by definition
\mathbb{E}T_{i}=0
.
Consider

\displaystyle Var[\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}]-(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}.

(S2)

Here

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{2}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)^{2}\int\frac{(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\int K^{2}(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta})f(x|y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

(S3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)^{2}\int\frac{(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{\delta}\int K^{2}(u)f(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)^{2}\int(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}\int K^{2}(u)(f(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})+u\delta f^{\prime}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})+o(\delta))

\displaystyle duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}(1+o(1))),

where the second equality is due to a change of variable, and the third equality is due to Assumption
1
. Meanwhile, we know

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int\frac{(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})}{\delta}\int K(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta})f(x|y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

(S4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})\int K(u)f(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})\int K(u)\frac{(u\delta)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\bigg(f^{(\ell)}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|y,\bm{z})+\mathcal{O}((u\delta)^{\zeta})\bigg)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{\ell}\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{b}^{*}(1+o(1)).

This together with Assumption
5
implies that
Var[\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})]=\frac{1}{\delta}\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}(1+o(1))
and therefore
Var(T_{i})=1+o(1).
Now we verify the Lyapunov condition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\sum_{i}^{n}\mathbb{E}|T_{i}|^{3}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\sum_{i}^{n}\mathbb{E}\bigg|\frac{\sqrt{\delta}\bm{v}^{*T}(\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))}{\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}}\bigg|^{3}.

(S5)

By Assumption
5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg|\frac{\sqrt{\delta}\bm{v}^{*T}(\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))}{\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}}\bigg|^{3}\lesssim

\displaystyle\delta^{3/2}\mathbb{E}\bigg|\bm{v}^{*T}(\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))\bigg|^{3}

(S6)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\delta^{3/2}(\mathbb{E}|\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})|^{3}+|\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})|^{3}),

where we can show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})|^{3}\leq

\displaystyle||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}^{3}\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}\int\delta\max_{j}\bigg|\frac{y\bm{z}_{j}}{\delta}K(u)\bigg|^{3}f(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z},y,\bm{z})dud\bm{z}

(S7)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}^{3}M_{n}^{3}/\delta^{2},

and from Lemma
S2
it’s easy to see that the first term on the RHS of (
S6
) is dominant. This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\sum_{i}^{n}\mathbb{E}|T_{i}|^{3}=\mathcal{O}((||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}M_{n})^{3}/(n\delta)^{1/2}).

(S8)

Therefore, under the condition of this Lemma the Lyapunov condition holds, which completes the proof by applying Lindeberg Feller Central Limit Theorem.

S8.2
Proof of Theorem
1

It suffices to show that
(n\delta)^{1/2}|\widehat{S}_{\delta}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0})-S_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
where
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}=(0,\widehat{\gamma})
. Here we only show that
(n\delta)^{1/2}|\widehat{S}_{\delta}^{(1)}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-S_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
and the desired result shall follow naturally from Lemma
1
.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}|\widehat{S}_{\delta}^{(1)}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-S_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})|

(S9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}|\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)T}\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\bm{\beta}^{*})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}|\bm{v}^{*T}(\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))|+(n\delta)^{1/2}|(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}-\bm{v}^{*})\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})|

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle I_{1}+I_{2}.

The fact that
\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})=0
and Lemma
S5
implies that

\displaystyle I_{1}=

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}|\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\bm{\gamma}}R^{n_{(1)}}_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{*})|+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(S10)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\big(||\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\bm{\gamma}}R^{n_{(1)}}_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\bm{\gamma}}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})||_{\max}+||\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\bm{\gamma}}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})||_{\max}\big)||\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{*}||_{1}

\displaystyle+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\frac{\eta_{1}(n)}{\delta}(\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}+\delta^{\ell})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Similarly, since
\nabla R(\bm{\beta}^{*})=0
, imply that

\displaystyle I_{2}=(n\delta)^{1/2}|(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}-\bm{v}^{*})(\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\nabla R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))|

(S11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}||\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}-\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}||\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\nabla R(\bm{\beta}^{*})||_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(n\delta)^{1/2}||\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}-\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\bigg(||\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\nabla R_{\delta}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})||_{\infty}

\displaystyle+||\nabla R_{\delta}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\nabla R(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})||_{\infty}+||\nabla R(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\nabla R(\bm{\beta}^{*})||_{\infty}\bigg).

Since
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)}
depends on the set of samples that is disjoint with
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, Lemma
S3
together with Lemma
S11
implies that

||\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})-\nabla R_{\delta}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{(2)})||_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}).

This in combine with Lemma
S2
and
S4
further implies that

I_{2}\lesssim(n\delta)^{1/2}||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}\eta_{2}(n)\bigg(M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}+\delta^{\ell}\bigg)=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Putting all above together with the results from Lemma
S9
and Lemma
S10
as well as the Slutsky’s theorem, we obtain that the bias corrected decorrelated score statistic
\widehat{U}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(0,1)
under the null hypothesis.

S8.3
Proof of Theorem
2

In the following, we shall prove the following general version of Theorem
2
.

Theorem S5
.

Under the conditions in Theorem
1
and Lemmas
S9
and
S10
in the supplemental materials, we further assume

\displaystyle

\displaystyle||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}^{2}M_{n}^{4}n^{1-4\phi}/\delta=o(1),\quad(n\delta)^{1/2}||\bm{v}^{*}||_{1}(\eta_{1}(n)\vee\eta_{2}(n))M_{n}n^{-\phi}=o(1),

(S12)

and
\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\sigma}
are consistent estimators of
\mu^{*},\sigma^{*}
. Then the following results hold under the local alternative hypothesis
H_{1}:\theta^{*}=\widetilde{C}n^{-\phi}
.

•

If
\widetilde{C}(n\delta)^{1/2}n^{-\phi}\nabla^{2}_{\theta|\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\sigma^{*-1}\rightarrow\xi
for some constant
\xi
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}N(-\xi,1).

(S13)

•

If
\widetilde{C}(n\delta)^{1/2}n^{-\phi}\nabla^{2}_{\theta|\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\sigma^{*-1}\rightarrow\infty
, then for any fixed
t
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(|\widehat{U}_{n}|>t)=1.

(S14)

Proof: Recall that
\widehat{S}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}})=\frac{1}{2}\big(\widehat{S}^{(1)}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})+\widehat{S}^{(2)}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(1)})\big)
. Let’s focus on
\widehat{S}^{(1)}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})
. By definition

\displaystyle\widehat{S}^{(1)}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})=\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)T}\nabla R_{\delta}^{n_{(1)}}(0,\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}^{(2)})

(S15)

\displaystyle=
