\displaystyle\left|\bm{v}^{*T}\left(\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n_{(j)}}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(k)}\right)-\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n_{(j)}}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)-\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n_{(j)}}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(k)}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)\right|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}^{2}\eta_{1}(n)^{2}}{\delta^{*2}}\right),

and

\displaystyle\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\left|\bm{v}^{*T}\left(\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n_{(j)}}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(k)}\right)-\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n_{(j)}}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)-\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n_{(j)}}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(k)}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\|\bm{v^{*}}\|_{1}M_{n}^{2}\eta_{1}(n)^{2}C_{n,\delta^{*}}}{\delta^{*\frac{1}{2}}}\right).

Under the conditions of Theorem
S7
, the desired result holds.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem
S7
.

Proof.

Recall that
\widehat{U}_{n}(\delta)=\sqrt{n\delta}\left(\frac{\widehat{S}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\delta^{\ell}\widehat{\mu}}{\widehat{\sigma}}\right)
. Denote
f(\delta)=\sqrt{n\delta}\left(\widehat{S}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\delta^{\ell}\widehat{\mu}\right)
. Note that the optimal bandwidth
\delta^{*}
has the same order as the fixed oracle
\delta
, hence it suffices to show that
f(\widehat{\delta})-f(\delta^{*})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
. Recall that to make
\widehat{\delta}
and the data in the score function independent, we apply the same cross-fitting technique. In the following proof, we omit the superscript for the
i
th fold for simplicity.

Write
f(\delta)=\sqrt{n\delta}\widehat{S}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\sqrt{n\delta}\delta^{\ell}\widehat{\mu}\coloneqq f_{1}(\delta)+f_{2}(\delta)
. In Lemma
S14
we showed that

\left|f_{1}(\widehat{\delta})-f_{1}(\delta^{*})-f_{1}^{\prime}(\delta^{*})(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\right|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Note that

\left|f_{2}(\widehat{\delta})-f_{2}(\delta^{*})-f_{2}^{\prime}(\delta^{*})(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{n}\tilde{\delta}^{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}\delta^{*2}C_{n,\delta^{*}}^{2}\widehat{\mu}\right),

where
\tilde{\delta}
is between
\delta^{*}
and
\widehat{\delta}
. Note that in Lemma
S9
we have shown that
|\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{*}|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, since
\mu^{*}
is bounded away from 0 and
\infty
, we have
\sqrt{n}\tilde{\delta}^{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}\delta^{*2}C_{n,\delta^{*}}^{2}\widehat{\mu}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\delta^{*\ell+\frac{1}{2}}C_{n,\delta^{*}}^{2}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
under the conditions of Theorem
S7
.

For simplicity we write
\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta})=\nabla_{\delta}(\nabla R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}))
, we can write

\displaystyle f(\widehat{\delta})-f(\delta^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\delta^{*})(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*}}{2\delta^{*}}\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}\left(\widehat{S}_{\delta^{*}}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\delta^{*\ell}\widehat{\mu}\right)+\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}\left(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\ell\delta^{*\ell-1}\widehat{\mu}\right)(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

In Theorem
1
, we showed that
\sqrt{n\delta}\left(\widehat{S}_{\delta}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\delta^{\ell}u^{*}\right)=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
for the oracle
\delta^{*}
. Then under the assumption that
|\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{*}|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, we have
\frac{\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*}}{2\delta^{*}}\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}\left(\widehat{S}_{\delta^{*}}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\delta^{*\ell}\widehat{\mu}\right)=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.

Denote

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))-\ell\delta^{*\ell-1}\widehat{\mu},

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\mathbb{E}(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})),

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*}).

Then we can write

\displaystyle\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}\left(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-\ell\delta^{*\ell-1}\widehat{\mu}\right)(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})=\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\left(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}\right).

Note that
\mathbb{E}(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))=\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}(\bm{\beta}^{*})
and

\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla R_{\delta}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)=\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int\left(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\int K(u)f\left(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z},

hence

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int\left(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\int K(u)uf^{\prime}\left(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int\left(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\int K(u)\frac{u^{\ell}\delta^{\ell-1}}{(\ell-1)!}\left(f^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left((u\delta)^{\zeta}\right)\right)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=\ell\delta^{\ell-1}\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{b}^{*}(1+o(1))=\ell\delta^{\ell-1}\mu^{*}(1+o(1)).

(S50)

Under the assumption that
|\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{*}|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
,
\delta^{*}\asymp n^{-1/(2\ell+1)}
and
\frac{\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*}}{\delta^{*}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, we have

\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})I_{1}=\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\ell\delta^{*(\ell-1)}(\mu^{*}-\widehat{\mu}+o(\mu^{*}))\asymp n^{\frac{1}{2\ell+1}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Next we consider
I_{2}
. First write
\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)
. Denote

T_{ij}=\left(\nabla_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)_{j},

then
\mathbb{E}(T_{ij})=0
. Note that

\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)=w\left(y_{i}\right)\frac{y_{i}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}K\left(\frac{y_{i}\left(x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i}\right)}{\delta}\right),

hence

\nabla_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)=-w(y_{i})\frac{y_{i}\bm{z_{i}}}{\delta^{2}}K\left(\frac{x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}\right)-w(y_{i})\frac{y_{i}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}K^{\prime}\left(\frac{x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}\right)\frac{x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left((\nabla_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))_{j}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y}\int\left(w(y)\frac{yz_{j}}{\delta^{2}}K\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta}\right)+w(y)\frac{yz_{j}}{\delta}K^{\prime}\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta}\right)\frac{y\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{2}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y}\int\left(w(y)^{2}\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{\delta^{3}}K^{2}(u)+w(y)^{2}\frac{z_{j}^{2}u^{2}}{\delta^{3}}K^{\prime 2}(u)+2w(y)^{2}y\frac{z_{j}^{2}u}{\delta^{3}}K(u)K^{\prime}(u)\right)f(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y}\int\left(w(y)^{2}\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{\delta^{3}}K^{2}(u)+w(y)^{2}\frac{z_{j}^{2}u^{2}}{\delta^{3}}K^{\prime 2}(u)+2w(y)^{2}y\frac{z_{j}^{2}u}{\delta^{3}}K(u)K^{\prime}(u)\right)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~\cdot\left(f(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})+u\delta f^{\prime}(\tau u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})\right)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z},

where
0<\tau<1
.
Note that

\int K^{2}(u)du,\int|u|K^{2}(u)du,\int u^{2}K^{\prime}(u)^{2}du,\int|u^{3}|K^{\prime}(u)^{2}du

are bounded, hence
\mathbb{E}\left((\nabla_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))_{j}^{2}\right)=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\delta^{3}})
.
From (
S50
) we know that

\left(\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)_{j}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\ell-2}).

(S51)

Therefore
\mathbb{E}T_{ij}^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\right)
. Since
\left|T_{ij}\right|\lesssim\frac{M_{n}^{2}}{\delta^{3}}
, by Bernstein inequality with
M_{n}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n\delta^{3}}}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, we can show that with probability greater than
1-o(1)

\|\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n\delta^{3}}}.

(S52)

Therefore with probability greater than
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle|\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})I_{2}|=

\displaystyle|\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\left(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\mathbb{E}(\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*}))\right)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})|\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\|\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\log d}\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\left|\frac{\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*}}{\delta^{*}}\right|=o(1)

under the conditions in Theorem
S7
.

Now we analyze
I_{3}
. Write

\displaystyle\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})I_{3}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}\left(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0})-\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})|\bm{v}^{*T}\left(\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0})-\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)|

\displaystyle~~+\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})|(\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}-\bm{v}^{*T})\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0})|

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle I_{31}+I_{32}.

By Lemma
S15
we have

\displaystyle I_{31}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})|\bm{v}^{*T}\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}-\bm{\beta}^{*})|+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\|\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Denote

T_{ijk}=\left(\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)_{jk}.

Note that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)=2w(y_{i})\frac{y_{i}\bm{z_{i}}\bm{z_{i}}^{T}}{\delta^{3}}K^{\prime}\left(\frac{x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}\right)+w(y_{i})K^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}\right)\frac{y_{i}(x_{i}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i})}{\delta^{4}}\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{i}^{T},

and we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})_{jk}=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int z_{j}z_{k}\int K(u)uf^{\prime\prime}\left(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)y\int z_{j}z_{k}\int K(u)\frac{u^{\ell-1}\delta^{\ell-2}}{(\ell-2)!}\left(f^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left((u\delta)^{\zeta}\right)\right)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\ell-2}),

hence

\displaystyle\left(\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)_{jk}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\ell-4}).

Note that

\int K^{\prime}(u)^{2}du.\int|u|K^{\prime}(u)^{2}du,\int u^{2}K^{\prime\prime}(u)^{2}du,\int|u^{3}|K^{\prime\prime}(u)^{2}du

are bounded, hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left(\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)_{jk}^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y}\int\left(2w(y)\frac{yz_{j}z_{k}}{\delta^{3}}K^{\prime}\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta}\right)+w(y)K^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta}\right)\frac{y(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})}{\delta^{4}}z_{j}z_{k}\right)^{2}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y}\int w(y)^{2}\frac{z_{j}^{2}z_{k}^{2}}{\delta^{5}}\left(4K^{\prime}(u)^{2}+u^{2}K^{\prime\prime}(u)^{2}+4uK^{\prime\prime}(u)K^{\prime}(u)\right)f(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y}\int w(y)^{2}\frac{z_{j}^{2}z_{k}^{2}}{\delta^{5}}\left(4K^{\prime}(u)^{2}+u^{2}K^{\prime\prime}(u)^{2}+4uK^{\prime\prime}(u)K^{\prime}(u)\right)

\displaystyle~~~\cdot\left(f(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})+u\delta f^{\prime}(\tau u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})\right)duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\delta^{5}}).

Therefore
\mathbb{E}T_{ijk}^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{5}}\right)
.
Since
\left|T_{ijk}\right|\lesssim\frac{M_{n}^{3}}{\delta^{4}}
, applying Bernstein inequality with
\frac{M_{n}^{3}}{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n\delta}}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, we can show that with probability greater than
1-o(1)

\|\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n\delta^{5}}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}+\max\left(\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)_{jk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\ell-2})+\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n\delta^{5}}}),

and with probability greater than
1-o(1)

\displaystyle I_{31}\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\|\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n}\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}C_{n,\delta^{*}}\delta^{*\ell}}{\sqrt{\delta^{*}}}\eta_{1}(n)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

under the conditions in Theorem
S7
, where
\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\eta_{1}(n)
is from Assumption
6
.

For
I_{32}
, by Lemma
S15
we have

\displaystyle I_{32}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\|\widehat{\bm{v}}-\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\|\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\|\widehat{\bm{v}}-\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\left(\|\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}+\|\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\right)

In the proof for
I_{31}
we have showed that
\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\|\nabla^{2}_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.
By (
S51
) and (
S52
) we have
\|\nabla_{\cdot\delta}R_{\delta^{*}}^{n}(\bm{\beta}^{*})\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\delta^{*\ell-1})
, and from Assumption
6
we know that
\|\widehat{\bm{v}}-\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}\eta_{2}(n)
, hence we have

\displaystyle I_{32}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}\eta_{2}(n)\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}(\widehat{\delta}-\delta^{*})\delta^{*\ell-1})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{n\delta^{*}}\eta_{2}(n)\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}C_{n,\delta^{*}}\delta^{*\ell})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

under the conditions in Theorem
S7
.
Combine the result we obtained for
I_{1},I_{2}
and
I_{3}
, the desired result holds.
∎

S10
More Details on Conditions and Assumptions

S10.1
About existence and uniqueness of
\beta^{*}

Recall that the risk function
R(\bm{\beta})
is

R(\bm{\beta})=\mathbb{E}\big[w(Y)L_{01}\{Y(X-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z})\},

where
L_{01}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\{1-\text{sign}(u)\}
is the 0-1 loss and
w(Y)
is a known weight. Unfortunately, the function
R(\bm{\beta})
is not always convex. The following is a counter-example. Suppose the distribution of
X
given
Y
is
X\mid Y\sim N(Y,1)
. For simplicity, we assume there is no covariate
\bm{Z}
and therefore we use a common threshold
\gamma
to dichotomize
X
. Let the weight
w(1)=1/\pi,w(-1)=1/(1-\pi)
, where
\pi=\mathbb{P}(Y=1)
, then the risk function becomes

\displaystyle(1.2)

\displaystyle=R(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}\left[w(Y)L_{01}\left\{Y\left(X-\gamma\right)\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\gamma}f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y=1)dx+\int_{\gamma}^{\infty}f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y=-1)dx.

Hence

\nabla R(\gamma)=f_{X\mid Y}(\gamma\mid y=1)-f_{X\mid Y}(\gamma\mid y=-1)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(e^{-\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{2}}-e^{-\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{2}}\right),

\nabla^{2}R(\gamma)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left[-(\gamma-1)e^{-\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{2}}+(\gamma+1)e^{-\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{2}}\right].

Note that for
2<\gamma<3
,
\nabla^{2}R(\gamma)<0
, hence in this case the risk function is not convex.

Given the fact that the risk function is generally not convex, a more subtle question is when
\bm{\beta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\beta}}R(\bm{\beta})
exists and is unique.
Before we elaborate this point, we first note that the existence and uniqueness of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
is a standard identifiability assumption, commonly used in the M-estimation literature. Otherwise, the estimand
\bm{\beta}^{*}
is not well defined. In the following, we will discuss the existence and uniqueness of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
respectively.

Existence
. The existence of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
can be verified under more specific modeling assumptions. For example,
Manski 1985
considered the following binary response model

Y=\operatorname{sign}\left(X-\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\beta}^{*}+\epsilon\right),

(S1)

where
\operatorname{Median}(\epsilon\mid X,\bm{Z})=0
. It is shown that the true coefficient
\bm{\beta}^{*}
is a minimizer of the risk function
R(\bm{\beta})
with equal weight, and therefore the minimizer of the risk exists. Note that this is the data generating model used in our simulation studies. In addition, we can also show that the minimizer of the risk also exists under the logistic regression and linear discriminant analysis, we refer to
Feng et al. 2022
for more details.

Uniqueness
. Generally speaking, showing the uniqueness of the minimizer under a non-convex loss is a very challenging problem. In the following, we leverage the symmetric property of the loss to show the uniqueness of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
under the binary response model (
S1
).

In this model, we assume
\epsilon\mid X,\bm{Z}\sim N(0,1)
, and
(X,\bm{Z})
is multivariate normal with covariance matrix
\Sigma
and mean 0. Suppose
w(1)=w(-1)=1
.
Note that

f(x\mid y=-1,\bm{z})=\frac{F_{\epsilon\mid x,\bm{z}}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}-x\mid x,\bm{z}\right)f_{x\mid\bm{z}}(x\mid\bm{z})}{\mathbb{P}(Y=-1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})},

and

f(x\mid y=1,\bm{z})=\frac{F_{\epsilon\mid x,\bm{z}}\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid x,\bm{z}\right)f_{x\mid\bm{z}}(x\mid\bm{z})}{\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})},

where
F_{\epsilon\mid x,\bm{z}}
is the cdf of
\epsilon
given
X,\bm{Z}
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\nabla R(\bm{\beta})=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\int yzf\left(\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\bm{z}\left(F_{\epsilon\mid x,\bm{z}}\left(\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid x,\bm{z}\right)-F_{\epsilon\mid x,\bm{z}}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid x,\bm{z}\right)\right)f_{x\mid\bm{z}}(\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z})f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1+d/2}|\Sigma|^{1/2}}\int\bm{z}\int_{(\bm{\beta}^{*}-\bm{\beta})^{T}\bm{z}}^{(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)dt\exp\left(-\frac{(\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z})^{T}\Sigma^{-1}(\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z})}{2}\right)d\bm{z}.

Then by the mean value theorem, we have for any
\bm{\beta}\neq\bm{\beta}^{*}
there exists some
0<c<1
,

\displaystyle R(\bm{\beta})-R(\bm{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla R(c\bm{\beta}+(1-c)\bm{\beta}^{*})(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1+d/2}|\Sigma|^{1/2}}\int(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}\int_{c(\bm{\beta}^{*}-\bm{\beta})^{T}\bm{z}}^{c(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)dt\exp\left(-\frac{(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z})^{T}\Sigma^{-1}(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z})}{2}\right)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{1+d/2}|\Sigma|^{1/2}}\int_{(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}>0}(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}\int_{c(\bm{\beta}^{*}-\bm{\beta})^{T}\bm{z}}^{c(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)dt\exp\left(-\frac{(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z})^{T}\Sigma^{-1}(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z})}{2}\right)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

where
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}=c\bm{\beta}+(1-c)\bm{\beta}^{*}
. The key is the third equality, where we use the fact that the above function is symmetry in
\bm{z}
. Since we have
R(\bm{\beta})>R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
for any
\bm{\beta}\neq\bm{\beta}^{*}
, hence
\bm{\beta}^{*}
is unique.

S10.2
About Nikol’ski class

Recall that the Nikol’ski class is defined as the set of functions
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
whose derivatives
f^{(\ell)}
of order
\ell=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
exist and satisfy

\left[\int\left(f^{(\ell)}(x+t)-f^{(\ell)}(x)\right)^{2}dx\right]^{1/2}\leq L|t|^{\beta-\ell},\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

After going through the proof carefully, we realize that we can relax the Hölder class condition defined in (3.1) to a Nikol’ski class condition. That is, we assume
f^{(\ell)}(x\mid y,\bm{z})
satisfies the following Nikol’ski class constraint:

\left[\int\left(f^{(\ell)}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\triangle_{\bm{z}}\mid y,\bm{z})-f^{(\ell)}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})\right)^{2}d\bm{z}\right]^{1/2}\leq L\triangle^{\zeta},

(S2)

for any
|\triangle_{\bm{z}}|\leq\triangle
, where
\triangle_{\bm{z}}
may depend on
\bm{z}
and
L
is a constant. The condition (
S2
) looks a bit unconventional, because we need to allow
\triangle_{\bm{z}}
to depend on
\bm{z}
. The reason is as follows. In the proof of Lemma
S9
on the consistency of the bias estimate, a key step is to bound
\|T^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)-T^{(\ell)}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)})\|_{\infty}
, which is

\displaystyle\left\|T^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)-T^{(\ell)}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}\right)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\int y\bm{z}\left(f^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)-f^{(\ell)}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)\right)f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{j}\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\sqrt{\int z_{j}^{2}f^{2}(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}}\sqrt{\int\left(f^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)-f^{(\ell)}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)\right)^{2}d\bm{z}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{j}\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\sqrt{\int z_{j}^{2}f^{2}(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}}\cdot L|M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)|^{\zeta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left(M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)\right)^{\zeta}\right),

where the first inequity is from Cauchy inequality, and the second one is from the Nikol’ski condition (
S2
) with
\triangle_{\bm{z}}=(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}
and
\triangle=CM_{n}\eta_{1}(n)
as
\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\eta_{1}(n)
and
\|\bm{z}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{n}
. In this case, we have to allow the increment
\triangle_{\bm{z}}
to depend on
\bm{z}
to make the proof work. Since in (
S2
) the integration is over
\bm{z}
, the right hand of (
S2
) cannot depend on
\bm{z}
and therefore we introduce an upper bound
\triangle
with
|\triangle_{\bm{z}}|\leq\triangle
in (
S2
).

In conclusion, our theory does work under a more relaxed Nikol’ski class condition (
S2
). However, since (
S2
) deviates from the standard Nikol’ski condition in a very subtle way, to avoid any confusion, we decide to keep the Hölder class in the main paper and include the above discussions on the Nikol’ski condition in the supplementary materials.

S10.3
Examples of kernel functions

In Table
S10.1
below, we provide a list of commonly-seen second-order, fourth-order and sixth-order kernel functions.

Table S10.1:
Commonly-seen second-order, fourth-order and sixth-order kernel functions
K(\cdot)
.

Equation
K(\cdot)

Uniform

K_{0}(t)=\frac{1}{2}I(|t|\leq 1)

K_{1}(t)=\frac{3}{4}(1-t^{2})I(|t|\leq 1)

S10.4
Verifying Assumptions under Binary Response Models

In the section, we articulate the assumptions used in Section
3
under the binary response model. The following lemma is our main result.

Lemma S16
.

Consider the following binary response model
Y=\operatorname{sign}\left(X-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{Z}+\epsilon\right)
, where we assume
(X,\bm{Z})
has mean
0
and
\epsilon\sim N\left(0,\sigma^{2}\right)
. Assume that the smallest and largest eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{Z}:=\Cov(\bm{Z})
are bounded away from 0 and infinity by some constants. Under either of the following two sets of conditions: for some constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, (1)
\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\leq C_{1}
,
X\mid\bm{Z}\sim N(\bm{c}^{T}\bm{Z},\sigma_{X}^{2})
, with
\|\bm{c}\|_{1}\leq C_{1}
and
Z_{j}
has a bounded support uniformly over
j
, (2)
\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}\leq C_{2}
and
X\sim N(0,\sigma_{X}^{2})
independent of
\bm{Z}\sim N(0,\bm{\Sigma}_{Z})
, our Assumptions
1
-
5
hold.

For the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
in Assumption
6
, it is established by
Feng et al. 2022
. Specifically, Assumption 3.1 and 3.2 in
Feng et al. 2022
are the same as Assumption 1 and Assumption 4 in this paper. Verification of Assumption 3.3 and 3.4 for binary response model is shown in supplementary material for
Feng et al. 2022
section S.4 and S.3 respectively. Finally, we note that the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{v}}
in Assumption
6
is established in Lemma
S8
in the supplementary material under Assumptions
1
-
5
, and
\lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))\geq c
for some constant
c>0
. The latter condition is an intermediate step in the proof of Lemma
S16
and therefore also holds.

Proof.

Denote
f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(x\mid\bm{z})
as the Gaussian p.d.f of
X
given
\bm{Z}
,
\Phi(\cdot)
as the c.d.f of the standard Gaussian distribution. Note that

\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid X=x,\bm{Z}=\bm{z})=\Phi\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\sigma}\right),

hence

f_{X\mid Y,\bm{Z}}(x\mid y=1,\bm{z})=\frac{\Phi\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\sigma}\right)f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(x\mid\bm{z})}{\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})}.

(S3)

As Gaussian p.d.f is infinitely differentiable, for any integer
l\geq 1
, with some calculation we have

f^{(\ell)}_{X\mid Y,\bm{Z}}(x\mid y=1,\bm{z})=\frac{\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\ell\\
k\end{array}\right)\Phi^{(k)}\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\sigma}\right)f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}^{(\ell-k)}(x\mid\bm{z})}{\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})}.

Note that the derivatives of Gaussian p.d.f are continuous and bounded, hence for any fixed integer
\ell\geq 2
, they are uniformly bounded. Therefore,
\left|\Phi^{(i)}\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\sigma}\right)\right|,\left|f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}^{(i)}(x\mid\bm{z})\right|
are bounded above by some constant for almost all
\bm{z}
for
i=0,\ldots,l
. In addition, we can show that
\mathbb{P}(Y=1)=0.5
and

\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})=\int_{X}\Phi\left(\frac{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\sigma}\right)f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(x\mid\bm{z})dx,

Let us first consider case (1). When
\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1},\|\bm{c}\|_{1}\leq C_{1}
and
Z_{j}
has a bounded support, we know that
|\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|,|\bm{c}^{T}\bm{z}|\leq C
for some constant
C
. Thus,

\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})\geq\int_{X}\Phi\left(\frac{x-C}{\sigma}\right)f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(x\mid\bm{z})dx\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(X>C|\bm{Z}=\bm{z}),

which is lower bounded by a positive constant for any
\bm{z}
. This implies that

f(x\mid y,\bm{z})
is
\ell
smooth, and Assumptions
1
and
3
are satisfied. Since
Z_{j}
is bounded, Assumption
4
is also true.

To show that
f(x\mid y,\bm{z})
is
\ell
smooth under case (2), consider
X\sim N(0,1)
without loss of generality. Denote

\phi(\cdot)
as the p.d.f of the standard Gaussian distribution.
Then

f^{(\ell)}_{X\mid Y,\bm{Z}}(x\mid y=1,\bm{z})=\frac{\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\ell\\
k\end{array}\right)\Phi^{(k)}\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\phi^{(\ell-k)}(x)}{\int\Phi\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\phi(x)dx}.

Note that
\Phi\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt
,
\Phi^{(k)}\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)=g(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})\exp(-\frac{(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{2})
, where

g(\cdot)
is some polynomial function. Hence for any integer
0\leq k\leq\ell
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\Phi^{(k)}\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right))}{\partial(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})}=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})}{\partial(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})}\exp(-\frac{(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{2})+g(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})\exp(-\frac{(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{2})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle h(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})\exp(-\frac{(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{2}),

where
h(\cdot)
is also some polynomial function.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{|\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\Phi^{(k)}\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\phi^{(\ell-k)}(x)}{\int\Phi\left(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\phi(x)dx}=\lim_{|\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|\rightarrow\infty}\frac{h(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})\exp(-\frac{(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{2})\phi^{(\ell-k)}(x)}{-\int\exp{(-\frac{(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}+x^{2}}{2})}dx}.

(S4)

Note that

\displaystyle\int\exp{(-\frac{(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}+x^{2}}{2})}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\exp{(-\frac{1}{4}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2})}\int\exp{-\frac{(y-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{2}}dy=C\exp{(-\frac{1}{4}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2})}

for some constant
C
. Hence,

\displaystyle(\ref{lim1})=C_{1}\lim_{|\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|\rightarrow\infty}h(x-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})\phi^{(\ell-k)}(x)\exp(-\frac{(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}-2x)^{2}}{4})\exp(\frac{x^{2}}{2})=0.

This implies that
\lim\limits_{|\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}|\rightarrow\infty}f^{(\ell)}_{X\mid Y,\bm{Z}}(x\mid y=1,\bm{z})=0
, and we already showed that when
Z_{j}
has a bounded support,
f^{(\ell)}_{X\mid Y,\bm{Z}}(x\mid y=1,\bm{z})
is bounded. Therefore, Assumption
1
is satisfied under case (2).

Finally, we focus on Assumption
5
. Recall that

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)^{2}\int\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}\int K(u)^{2}duf\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=\int K(u)^{2}du\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z})f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}.

Note that
0<\int K(u)^{2}du<\infty
and
X\mid\bm{Z}
is Gaussian, for some constants
0<c_{1}<C_{1}<\infty
, we have

c_{1}<f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z})<C_{1},

under case (1).

Since the smallest and largest eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{Z}=\Cov(\bm{Z})
are bounded away from 0 and infinity by some constants, if we can further show
\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{2}
is also bounded from above and below, then we have
\sigma^{*}=\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}
is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
.

Recall that
\bm{v}^{*}=\left(1,-\bm{\omega}^{*T}\right)^{T}
. So, clearly
\|\bm{v}\|_{2}\geq 1
. In the following, we focus on the upper bound of
\bm{\omega}^{*}
. Recall that
\bm{\omega}^{*}=\left(\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)
. We have

\frac{\|\bm{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\|_{2}^{2}}\leq\lambda_{\max}^{2}\left(\left(\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)^{-1}\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}^{2}\left((\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{-1}\right).

Note that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=\pm 1}w(y)\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}yf^{\prime}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y\right)f(\bm{z},y)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f_{\epsilon\mid x,\bm{z}}(0\mid x=\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z},\bm{z})f_{x\mid\bm{z}}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=2f_{\epsilon}(0)\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})f(\bm{z})d\bm{z},

hence together with the eigenvalue condition on
\operatorname{Cov}(\bm{Z})
, both
\lambda_{\max}^{2}\left((\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{-1}\right)
and
\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)
are bounded above by some constant.
Therefore,

\|\bm{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\|_{2}^{2}\lambda_{\max}^{2}\left((\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{-1}\right)\leq C

for some constant
C
.

For case (2), note that

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f_{X\mid\bm{Z}}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z})f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{1}{|2\pi\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}|^{1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}}{2}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\bm{z}^{T}\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}^{-1}\bm{z}}{2}\right)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{1}{|2\pi\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}|^{1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\bm{z}^{T}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}^{-1}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\right)\bm{z}}{2}\right)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}^{-1}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\right)^{-1}}{|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}|^{1/2}|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}^{-1}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}|^{1/2}},

where in the last step we use the integral of a normal distribution with variance
\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}^{-1}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\right)^{-1}
. Using the assumption that
\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}
is bounded, we can similarly prove that
\sigma^{*}=\sqrt{\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}}
is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
.

For the bias
\mu^{*}
in Assumption
5
, write

\displaystyle|\mu^{*}|

\displaystyle:=|\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{b}^{*}|=\Big|\bm{v}^{*T}\left(\int K(u)\frac{u^{\ell}}{\ell!}du\right)\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}w(y)\int y\bm{z}f^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)f(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}\Big|

\displaystyle=2\Big|\bm{v}^{*T}\left(\int K(u)\frac{u^{\ell}}{\ell!}du\right)\cdot\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\Big(f^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y=1,\bm{z}\right)\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})

\displaystyle~~~~-f^{(\ell)}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y=-1,\bm{z}\right)\mathbb{P}(Y=-1\mid\bm{Z}=\bm{z})\Big)f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\{\int(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}\Big\}^{1/2},

where we use the fact that
f^{(\ell)}(x\mid y,\bm{z})
is bounded, and the last step is from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Finally, from our previous proof,
\int(\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{z})^{2}f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}
is bounded by a constant. This completes the proof.
∎

S10.5
About the magnitude of
w^{*}
and
v^{*}

In general, we make assumptions on the magnitude of
\bm{\omega}^{*}
, such as
\bm{\omega}^{*}
is sparse or approximately sparse, in order to achieve the consistency of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
and, subsequently, the validity of the debias/decorrelated inference for high-dimensional models.
Note that
\bm{v}^{*}=(1,-\bm{\omega}^{*T})^{T}
, thus the discussions on the magnitude of
\bm{\omega}^{*}
and
\bm{v}^{*}
are equivalent.

Usually, the sparsity of
\bm{\omega}^{*}
can be verified under some additional independence assumption among covariates. In the following, we demonstrate this point under the binary response model.
Consider the binary response model
Y=\operatorname{sign}\left(X-\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{Z}+u\right)
. Assume
w(y)\equiv 1
,
u
is independent of
X,\bm{Z}
and
(X,\bm{Z})\sim N(0,\bm{\Sigma})
. Denote
f_{x\mid\bm{z}}(\cdot)
as the p.d.f of
X
given
\bm{Z}
and
f_{u}(\cdot)
as the p.d.f of
u
.
Note that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle=2\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f_{u}(0)f_{x\mid\bm{z}}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})f(\bm{z})d\bm{z}.

Our first assumption is that, given a subset of
\bm{Z}
denoted by
\bm{Z}_{S_{1}}
,
X
is independent of the rest of the covariates. Since
(X,\bm{Z})\sim N(0,\bm{\Sigma})
, this assumption simply says the first row of the precision matrix
\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}
is sparse. Recall that we denote the support set of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
by
S
. Thus, the function
f_{x\mid\bm{z}}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})
only depends on
\bm{Z}_{S_{2}}
, where
S_{2}=S_{1}\cup S
.

Our second assumption is that
\bm{Z}
is blockwise independent. To be precise, we assume there exists a small set
S_{3}
containing
Z_{1}
and
\bm{Z}_{S_{2}}
defined above such that
\bm{Z}_{S_{3}}\perp\bm{Z}_{S_{3}^{c}}
. Intuitively, we can find
S_{3}
by merging all the blocks that contain variables in
Z_{1}
and
\bm{Z}_{S_{2}}
.

Then by rearranging
\bm{Z}
we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2f_{u}(0)\int_{\bm{Z}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{z}_{S_{3}}\bm{z}_{S_{3}}^{T}&\bm{z}_{S_{3}}\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}}^{T}\\
\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}}\bm{z}_{S_{3}}^{T}&\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}}\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}}^{T}\end{array}\right)\cdot f_{x|z}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})f(\bm{z}_{S_{3}})f(\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}})d\bm{z}_{S_{3}}d\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2f_{u}(0)\int_{\bm{Z}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{z}_{S_{3}}\bm{z}_{S_{3}}^{T}&0\\
0&\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}}\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}}^{T}\end{array}\right)\cdot f_{x|z}(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z})f(\bm{z}_{S_{3}})f(\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}})d\bm{z}_{S_{3}}d\bm{z}_{S_{3}^{c}},

where the off-diagonal block is 0 because
\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}_{S_{3}^{c}})=0
. Since
\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
is a block-wise diagonal matrix,
\left(\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right)^{-1}
is also block-wise diagonal.

Note that previously we defined
\theta
as the coefficient of
\bm{Z}_{1}
,
\bm{\gamma}
as the coefficient of the rest components of
\bm{Z}
, and we have
\bm{\omega}^{*}=(\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{-1}\nabla_{\theta,\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
.
From the block matrix inversion we know that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})^{-1}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\nabla_{\theta,\theta}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})&\nabla_{\theta,\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\\
\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})&\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\end{array}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}~~~~~~~~~~~~~*&-\frac{1}{k}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})^{-1}\nabla_{\theta,\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\\
-\frac{1}{k}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})^{-1}&~~~~~~~~~~~~~**\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}~~~*&-\frac{1}{k}\bm{\omega}^{*}\\
-\frac{1}{k}\bm{\omega}^{*T}&~~~**\end{array}\right),

where
k=\nabla_{\theta,\theta}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})-\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\theta}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})\nabla_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})^{-1}\nabla_{\theta,\bm{\gamma}}^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
. Comparing this with (
S10.5
), we can conclude that
\bm{\omega}^{*}
is sparse and its support set is contained in
S_{3}
.

Finally, we note that under the assumption that the smallest and largest eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{Z}:=\Cov(\bm{Z})
are bounded away from 0 and infinity by some constants, we can show that
\|\bm{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\leq C
for some constant
C
(see the proof of Lemma
S16
above). If
|S_{3}|\leq s_{3}
, Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields

\|\bm{\omega}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim s_{3}^{1/2}
. Thus,
\|\bm{\omega}^{*}\|_{1}
at most is of order
s_{3}^{1/2}
.

S10.6
About sparse eigenvalue assumptions

To establish the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, we need the following so-called sparse eigenvalue assumption.

Assumption S9
.

Define the largest and smallest sparse eigenvalues as

\displaystyle\rho_{\max}=\sup\bigg\{\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta})\bm{v}:||\bm{v}||_{2}=1,||\bm{v}||_{0}\leq Cs,\bm{\beta}\in\Omega,||\bm{\beta}||_{0}\leq Cs\bigg\},

(S15)

\displaystyle\rho_{\min}=\inf\bigg\{\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta}^{n}(\bm{\beta})\bm{v}:||\bm{v}||_{2}=1,||\bm{v}||_{0}\leq Cs,\bm{\beta}\in\Omega,||\bm{\beta}||_{0}\leq Cs\bigg\},

(S16)

where
C
is constant,
s
is the sparsity level of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
, and
\Omega
is a suitable convex set containing the true parameter
\bm{\beta}^{*}
(e.g.
\Omega=\{\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}\leq R\}
for some
R>0
).
We assume that
c\leq\rho_{\min}\leq\rho_{\max}\leq 1/c
for some constant
0<c<1
.

We note that we need to introduce the set
\Omega
in the above definition, since as shown above the population risk
R(\bm{\beta})
is globally non-convex. Thus, we have to focus on the eigenvalue structure in a local neighborhood around the truth. Theorem 1 in
Feng et al. 2022
showed that under the current Assumptions 1-4 and the above sparse eigenvalue assumption, the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
attains the minimax-optimal rate with
\eta_{1}(n)=\sqrt{s}(\frac{s\log(d)}{n})^{\ell/(2\ell+1)}
. The same paper further verifies that the sparse eigenvalue assumption indeed holds, when the data are generated from the binary response model under some mild assumptions (e.g. the smallest and largest eigenvalues of
\Cov(\bm{Z})
are bounded away from 0 and infinity by constants).

S10.7
About
\|\beta^{*}\|_{2}

It is seen from Lemma
S16
that, when the data are generated by a binary response model, we need some conditions on the norm of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
(either
\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\leq C
or
\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}\leq C
for some constant
C
) in order to verify Assumptions 1-6 used in our main results. So, for the binary response model, we need to normalize
\bm{\beta}^{*}
according to either
L_{1}
or
L_{2}
norm.

To see the reason, let us consider the binary response model
Y=\operatorname{sign}\left(X-\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z}+u\right)
, where for simplicity we assume
X\sim N(0,1),\bm{Z}\sim N(0,\mathbf{I})
independent of
X
,
u
is independent of
X,\bm{Z}
and
w(y)\equiv 1.
Assume its density
f_{u}(\cdot)
is bounded by a constant. The median of
u
is 0.
By the definition of
R(\bm{\beta})
, it is easily shown that for any
\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1
,

\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\bm{v}=2\int\left(\bm{z}^{T}\bm{v}\right)^{2}f_{u}(0)\phi\left(\bm{\beta}^{*T}\bm{z}\right)\phi\left(z_{1}\right)\ldots\phi\left(z_{n}\right)d\bm{z},

where
\phi
is the pdf of
N(0,1)
. Since
f_{u}(\cdot)
is upper bounded by a constant
C
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\bm{v}

\displaystyle\leq 2C\int\left(\bm{z}^{T}\bm{v}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\bm{z}^{T}\left(\mathbf{I}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\right)\bm{z}}{2}\right)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=2C\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\left(\mathbf{I}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\right)^{-1}\bm{v}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}\left|\mathbf{I}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\right|^{1/2}},

where in the last step we use the integral of a normal distribution with variance
\left(\mathbf{I}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}\right)^{-1}
. Since the matrix
\mathbf{I}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\bm{\beta}^{*T}
has
d-1
eigenvalues 1 and 1 eigenvalue
1+\left\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, we obtain that

\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\bm{v}\leq\frac{2C}{(2\pi)^{1/2}\left(1+\left\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}}.

Thus, when
\left\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\right\|_{2}
is diverging,
\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\bm{v}
is of order
1/\left\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\right\|_{2}\rightarrow 0
. In other words, the population Hessian matrix
\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\beta}^{*})
shrinks to 0 in the operator norm. This leads to slower convergence rate of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
(see
Feng et al. 2022
) and also the asymptotic variance of the score test
\sigma^{*2}
may diverge to infinity. For this reason, we need to normalize
\bm{\beta}^{*}
in the simulation.

S11
An Alternative Variance Estimator

Recall that the numerator of the score statistic
\widehat{U}_{n}
can be written as

\widehat{S}_{{\delta}}(0,\widehat{\gamma})-{\delta}^{\ell}\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\nabla R_{{\delta}}^{n}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{0}\right)-{\delta}^{\ell}\gamma_{K,\ell}\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}\widehat{T}_{h,U}^{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}):=\widehat{\bm{v}}^{T}D_{{\delta},h}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\bm{v}})

where

D_{\delta,h}^{n}(\bm{\beta},\bm{v})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w\left(y_{i}\right)y_{i}\frac{\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta}M\left(x_{i},\bm{z}_{i};\bm{\beta}\right):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta}),

M\left(x_{i},\bm{z}_{i};\bm{\beta}\right)=K\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}-x_{i}}{\delta}\right)-\frac{\delta^{\ell+1}}{h^{\ell+1}}U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}-x_{i}}{h}\right).

Recall that Lemma
1
shows that the asymptotic variance of
(n\delta)^{1/2}S_{\delta}\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)
is
\sigma^{*2}=\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}
. Therefore, we can alternatively estimate the asymptotic variance

\delta\operatorname{Var}\left[\bm{v}^{T}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right]=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\left(w(Y)Y\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\delta}M(X,\bm{Z};\bm{\beta})\right)^{2}\right]-\delta\left[\mathbb{E}w(Y)Y\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\delta}M(X,\bm{Z};\bm{\beta})\right]^{2}

by replacing the expectations above with sample averages under
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\text{ and }\widehat{\bm{v}}
. Formally, denote

\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T},

\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)^{T},

and

M^{n}_{\delta,h}\left(\bm{\beta},\bm{v}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\bm{v}^{T}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{v}^{T}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)^{2}=\bm{v}^{T}\left(\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})+\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})\right)\bm{v},

then our kernel-free variance estimator is
\delta M^{n}_{\delta,h}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\bm{v}}\right)
.

Theorem S8
.

Under the conditions in Lemmas
S17
and
S18
and

\delta\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell+1}}{h^{\ell+1}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}+M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)=o(1),

we have

\left|\delta M^{n}_{\delta,h}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\bm{v}}\right)-\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}\right|=\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}^{2}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\eta_{2}(n)+(\delta\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell+1}}{h^{\ell+1}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}+M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)\right).

Compared to the convergence rate in Lemma
S10
, we can see that the rate of the kernel-free estimator above may have a much slower rate.

Proof.

Following Lemma
S17
, Lemma
S18
, and Lemma
S9
where we showed that

\left\|H(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})-H(\bm{\beta}^{*})\right\|_{\max}\lesssim M_{n}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1},

we can obtain

\left\|\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{k}H(\bm{\beta}^{*})}{\delta}\right\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(1\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{h^{\ell+1}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta^{3}}}+\frac{M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)}{\delta}\right).

Since
\left\|H\left(\bm{\beta}^{*}\right)\right\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, we have

\left\|\delta\left(\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\right)\right\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
when

\delta\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell+1}}{h^{\ell+1}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}+M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)=o(1).

By triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\left|\delta M^{n}_{\delta,h}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\bm{v}}\right)-\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}-\bm{v}^{*}\right\|_{1}^{2}\delta\left\|\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\right\|_{\max}+2\left\|\bm{v}^{*T}\delta\left(\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\right)\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\widehat{\bm{v}}^{(1)}-\bm{v}^{*}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left|\bm{v}^{*T}\left(\delta\left(\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\right)-\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}\right)\bm{v}^{*}\right|.

This implies that

\left|\delta M^{n}_{\delta,h}\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\bm{v}}\right)-\bm{v}^{*T}\bm{\Sigma}^{*}\bm{v}^{*}\right|=\|\bm{v}^{*}\|_{1}^{2}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\eta_{2}(n)+\delta\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell+1}}{h^{\ell+1}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}+M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)\right).

∎

Lemma S17
.

Under Assumptions 1-4 and conditions in Lemma
S9
, assume
\int U^{(l)2}(u)du<C_{1}<\infty
,
|U^{(\ell)}(u)|<C_{2}<\infty
for some constant
C_{1},C_{2}
, for any fixed
\bm{\beta}
, we have

\left\|\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)^{T}-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{k}H(\bm{\beta})}{\delta}\right\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}(1\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{h^{\ell+1}}).

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[w^{2}(Y)\frac{\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}}{\delta^{2}}M^{2}(X,\bm{Z};\bm{\beta})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}\right)\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}\right)^{T}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[w^{2}(Y)\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\frac{\delta^{2\ell}}{h^{2(\ell+1)}}\left(U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z}-X}{h}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}\right)\left(w(Y)\bm{Z}^{T}\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{h^{\ell+1}}U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z}-X}{h}\right)\right)\right].

In the proof of Lemma 1 we showed that

\max_{j,k}\left|\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}\right)\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}\right)^{T}\right]\right)_{jk}-\left(\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{k}H(\bm{\beta})}{\delta}\right)_{jk}\right|=\mathcal{O}(1).

If
\int U^{(l)2}(u)du<C_{1}<\infty
,
|U^{(\ell)}(u)|<C_{2}<\infty
for some constant
C_{1},C_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\left[w^{2}(Y)\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\frac{\delta^{2\ell}}{h^{2(\ell+1)}}\left(U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z}-X}{h}\right)\right)^{2}\right]\right)_{jk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}\delta^{2\ell}w^{2}(y)\int\frac{z_{j}z_{k}}{h^{2(1+\ell)}}\int U^{(\ell)2}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-x}{h}\right)f(x\mid y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}\delta^{2\ell}w^{2}(y)\int\frac{z_{j}z_{k}}{h^{2\ell+1}}\int U^{(\ell)2}\left(u\right)f(uh+\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\frac{\delta^{2\ell}}{h^{2\ell+1}}),

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}^{i}\left(\bm{\beta}\right)\left(w(Y)\bm{Z}^{T}\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{h^{\ell+1}}U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{Z}-X}{h}\right)\right)\right]\right)_{jk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}\delta^{\ell-1}w^{2}(y)\int\frac{z_{j}z_{k}}{h^{1+\ell}}\int K\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}-x_{i}}{\delta}\right)U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}_{i}-x_{i}}{h}\right)f(x\mid y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{-1,1\}}\delta^{\ell}w^{2}(y)\int\frac{z_{j}z_{k}}{h^{1+\ell}}\int K(u)U^{(\ell)}(\frac{u\delta}{h})f(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{h^{\ell+1}}).

Note that since
\frac{\delta}{h}=o(1)
, we have

\max_{j,k}\left|\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)\right)_{jk}-\left(\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{k}H(\bm{\beta})}{\delta}\right)_{jk}\right|=\mathcal{O}(1\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{h^{\ell+1}}).

Together with Lemma
S18
which shows that
\left\|\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{\ell}\right)
, we have,

\left\|\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)^{T}-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{k}H(\bm{\beta})}{\delta}\right\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}(1\vee\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{h^{\ell+1}}).

∎

Lemma S18
.

Under Assumptions 1-4 and conditions in Lemma
S9
, assume
|U^{(\ell)}(u)|<C<\infty
for some constant
C
,
\int K^{4}(t)dt<\infty
. For any fixed
\bm{\beta}
, we have

\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)\right]\right\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right),

\left\|\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{\ell}\right),

\left\|\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})+\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)^{T}\right\|_{\max}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta^{3}}}\right).

Proof.

Denote

\displaystyle\Sigma_{ijk}=

\displaystyle\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)\right)_{jk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{2}(y_{i})\frac{z_{ij}z_{ik}}{\delta^{2}}M^{2}\left(x_{i},\bm{z}_{i};\bm{\beta}\right)-\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)\right)_{jk}.

We have
\mathbb{E}(\Sigma_{ijk})=0
. Note that since
\frac{\delta}{h}=o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)_{jk}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[w^{4}(Y)\frac{z_{j}^{2}z_{k}^{2}}{\delta^{4}}M^{4}(X,\bm{Z};\bm{\beta})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int w^{4}(y)\frac{z_{j}^{2}z_{k}^{2}}{\delta^{4}}\left(K\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-x}{\delta}\right)-\frac{\delta^{\ell+1}}{h^{\ell+1}}U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-x}{h}\right)\right)^{4}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int w^{4}(y)\frac{z_{j}^{2}z_{k}^{2}}{\delta^{4}}K^{4}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-x}{\delta}\right)f(x\mid y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int w^{4}(y)\frac{z_{j}^{2}z_{k}^{2}}{\delta^{3}}K^{4}\left(u\right)f(u\delta+\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z})duf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}\right),

where in the third step, we ignore the term involving the fourth moment of
U^{(\ell)}(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-x}{h})
because it is of a smaller order. Similarly we can show that
\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)\right)_{jk}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
. Since
|\Sigma_{ijk}|\lesssim\frac{M_{n}^{2}}{\delta^{2}}
, by Bernstein inequality we can show that with probability greater than
1-\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-1}\right)
,

\left\|\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})^{T}\right)\right\|_{\max}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta^{3}}}.

Following the same proof of Lemma
S9
we can show that

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right)_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int w(y)\frac{z_{j}}{\delta}\left(K\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-x}{\delta}\right)-\frac{\delta^{\ell+1}}{h^{\ell+1}}U^{(\ell)}\left(\frac{\bm{\beta}^{T}\bm{z}-x}{h}\right)\right)f(x\mid y,\bm{z})dxf(y,\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{\ell}\right),

and with probability greater than
1-\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-1}\right)
,

\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}.

Therefore, with probability greater than
1-\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\Sigma}^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)^{T}\right\|_{\max}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})+\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right)^{T}\right\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right\|_{\infty}\left(2\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right)_{\infty}+\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})-\mathbb{E}\left(\bar{D}_{\delta,h}^{i}(\bm{\beta})\right)\right\|_{\infty}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}\vee\delta^{\ell}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n\delta}}.

Combining this with the result for
\Sigma^{n}_{\delta,h}(\bm{\beta})
we finish the proof.
∎

S12
Practical Considerations

In practice, in order to apply the smoothed decorrelated score test presented in Section
2.2
, we need to select the bandwidth and regularization parameter in the initial estimators
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, two bandwidths for the plug-in asymptotic bias and variance estimators in (
2.10
) and (
2.12
), and the final bandwidth
\delta
for the score test statistic. In this section, we discuss practical considerations for the proposed test as well as the data-driven selection procedure for
\delta
.

We use a path-following algorithm to compute the initial estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and apply a two way cross-validation approach to choose the tuning parameters
(\delta,\lambda)
in the optimization (
2.2
); see
Feng et al. 2022
for details. The initial values of the path-following algorithm used in the simulation are shown in Section
S13
.
To compute the Dantzig estimator
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
, we use the “flare” package in R. The same cross-validation method can be used to select the tuning parameters
(\delta,\lambda^{\prime})
in (
2.14
). Meanwhile, we find the empirical performance of the Dantzig estimator
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
is not very sensitive to the choice of
(\delta,\lambda^{\prime})
. In the simulation and real data analysis, to ease the computation, we set
\lambda^{\prime}=2(\log d/n)^{1/5}
and
\delta=1
in (
2.14
). Notice that in rare cases, the Dantzig solver may become unstable numerically when the Hessian
\nabla^{2}R^{n}_{\delta}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})
is ill-conditioned. In this case, we will firstly project it onto the cone of positive definite matrices and then plug-in the projected matrix into (
2.14
) to solve
\widehat{\bm{\omega}}
.

For the bandwidth parameter
h
in the plug-in bias estimator, Lemma
S9
implies that the theoretical optimal order for
h
is
(\log(d)/n)^{2\ell+2\zeta+1}
(if
M_{n}\eta_{1}(n)=o(h)
which holds under mild conditions). In practice, we recommend choosing
h=(\log(d)/n)^{2\ell^{\prime}+3}
, where
\ell^{\prime}
is the order of the kernel function
K
. For variance estimator, we use the kernel-free approach, as it is more convenient in practice.

Finally, for the main bandwidth parameter
\delta
appearing in the score test statistic, if a pre-specified value is preferred, we recommend choosing
\delta=cn^{-1/(2\ell^{\prime}+1)}
for some constant
c>0
(e.g.,
c=1
). When using
our proposed data-driven bandwidth selector, notice that another bandwidth parameter
b
, whose optimal order is
(\log(d)/n)^{1/(2\ell+2r+1)}
(see the discussion after Theorem
S6
), is required for estimating the squared bias
SB(\delta)
. Similarly, we suggest choosing
b=c_{0}(\log(d)/n)^{1/(2\ell^{\prime}+2r^{\prime}+1)}
for some
c_{0}
(e.g.,
c_{0}=0.5
), where
r^{\prime}
is the order of kernel
J
.

S13
Additional Numerical Results

S13.1
Computational details of the path-following algorithm

In Section
6
, we apply the path-following algorithm proposed in
Feng et al. 2022
to estimate the true coefficient
\bm{\beta}
. We fix the number of stages
N=25
,
\nu=0.25
,
\eta=0.25
,
\epsilon_{tgt}=0.0001
and set
\Omega=\{\bm{\beta}:||\bm{\beta}||_{2}\leq 10^{3}\}
for each parameter in Algorithm 2.1 and 2.2 in
Feng et al. 2022
.

S13.2
Normal Q-Q plots in Section
6.1

Figure
S13.1
contains the Normal Q-Q plots for the test statistics obtained from the experiments in Section
6.1
under both scenarios. We can see that the Q-Q plots from the proposed SDS method appear close to Gaussian, while the Q-Q plots for DS and Honest method deviate from standard Gaussian.

S13.3
Empirical power in Section
6.1

Figure
S13.2
shows the empirical power under the Heteroskedastic Uniform scenario in Section
6.1
. Similar to the figures for the Heteroskedastic Gaussian scenario, the empirical power converges to 1 as the magnitude of
\beta^{*}
becomes larger. In addition, as the correlation between
\bm{Z}
becomes larger, the power also decreases.

S13.4
Bandwidth selection in Section
6.2

Finally, we investigate whether the data-driven bandwidth is close to the theoretically optimal bandwidth that minimizes the MSE. We focus on the Heteroskedastic Gaussian scenario and consider the setting with
n=800,d=50,s=10,\rho=0.2
and set
\beta^{*}_{1}=\dotso=\beta^{*}_{10}=1/\sqrt{10}
. We compare
M(\delta)
with
\widehat{M}(\delta)
, where
\widehat{M}(\delta)
is obtained using the implementation discussed in Section
S12
, but with different bandwidth
b=0.15,0.2,0.25,0.3
for squared bias estimation. Notice that since directly obtaining
M(\delta)
is hard, we apply Monte Carlo integration to approximate this quantity.

Figure
S13.3
shows the true
M(\delta)
as well as
\widehat{M}(\delta)
in each case. The red line is the true MSE
M(\delta)
approximated by Monte Carlo method, and the black solid line is the average estimated MSE
\widehat{M}(\delta)
with different bandwidths
b
over 100 repetitions. The black dashed line is the one standard deviation band around
\widehat{M}(\delta)
over these repetitions.
As we can see, for all cases,
M(\delta)
lies within the one standard deviation band around
\widehat{M}(\delta)
and the minimizer of
M(\delta)

is very close to the minimizer of the average
\widehat{M}(\delta)
. This experiment confirms that the data-driven bandwidth
\widehat{\delta}
is close to the optimal
\delta^{*}
, provided that the bandwidth
b
for squared-bias estimation lies in a suitable range.

S13.5
Bandwidth selection in high dimension

We run the data-driven bandwidth selection procedure under the high dimensional setting. Specifically, we consider the same Heteroskedastic Gaussian and Heteroskedastic Uniform scenario with
n=300,d=500,s=3
, and
\beta^{*}_{0}\in\{0,0.05,\dotso,0.3\}
. Similar to Section
6.2
, we seek for the minimizer of the estimated MSE over
\delta\in[0.1,1.2]
, and then plug-in this estimated bandwidth
\widehat{\delta}
into the decorrelated score function. We repeat this experiment 250 times for each case. Table
S13.1
and Figure
S13.4
show the empirical Type-I error and empirical power, respectively. The performance for both Type-I error and power is similar as in the low-dimensional case.

S13.6
Sensitivity analysis

We run a set of experiments with different choices of kernels. We consider the experiments similar to Section 6.1 with
n=300,d\in\{100,500\}
and
s=3
. Instead of using Gaussian kernel in the decorrelated score function, we consider the following two choices:

•

Epanechnikov(parabolic) kernel:
K(u)=\frac{3}{4}(1-u^{2}),|u|\leq 1
,

•

Quartic (biweight) kernel:
K(u)=\frac{15}{16}(1-u^{2})^{2},|u|\leq 1
.

Table
S13.2
shows the empirical Type-I error using these two kernels under both low and high dimensional regions. We can see that the empirical Type-I errors are close to the nominal level 0.05, and seem not largely affected by the choice of kernels in this setting.

In the next set of experiments, we study how the choice of bandwidth affects the empirical Type-I error. Similar to the above experiments, we fix
n=300,d=100,s=3
, and consider the fixed bandwidth setting with
\delta\in\{0.3,0.5,0.75,1.0\}
in the decorrelated score function. The results are shown in Table
S13.3
. We can see that the empirical performance of our testing procedure using these bandwidth parameters is in general close to the nominal level 0.05.

Finally, we also examine how the bandwidth in the bias estimator influences the empirical performance of the score test. We run additional experiments with a range of different bandwidths in the plug-in bias estimator. Specifically, we consider the same Heteroskedastic Gaussian scenario with
n=300,d=100,500
and
s=3
. To estimate
\widehat{\mu}
, we set the bandwidth
h=0.5,0.8,1.0,1.2
in the plug-in estimator and evaluate the empirical Type-I error with 250 repeated experiments for each case. From Table
S13.4
, we can see that although the empirical Type-I errors vary with different bandwidths, they are generally around the nominal level 0.05.

S13.7
Additional results from ChAMP analysis

Table
S13.5
lists the five most significant variables using the DS and Honest approach. Notice that the significant variables identified by the proposed approach are also in the list for the DS approach. Honest yields very different set of variables compared to the other two.
